CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Royal Colonels-in-Chief. For the first time in the history of our Army, Royal ladies have been given rank as honorary colonels-in-chief of our finest regiments. Her Majesty the Queen was some time ago placed at the head of a famous Prussian regiment, the Pomeranian Hussars, who served under Blucher at Waterloo. The Kaiser in his vengeful anger at Britain's intervention in the war on behalf of Belgium severed his honorary associations with our Army and Navy. The Duke of Saxe- Coburg, the grandson of Queen Victoria, has also given up the colonelship-in-chief of the Sea- forth Highlanders because our troops have " shamelessly fallen upon Germany." Neither our soldiers nor our sailors will, however, be guilty of such barbarism as the Kaiser's army perpetrated at Louvain and elsewhere in Bel- gium. Queen Mary's own regiment is the 18th Hussars, whilst Queen Alexandra is colonel-in- chief of the 10th Hussars and of the West York- shire Regiment. The Duchess of Argyll is at the head of the Argyll and Sutherland Highland Regiments.

The Daughters of the Czar. The three elder daughters of the Czar of Russia are also colonels-in-chief of famous Rus- sian regiments. The Grand Duchess Olga and Marie rode in full uniform at the head of their respective men during the recent manoeuvres, when the Czar reviewed his troops, then en- camped at Krasnoe Selo. The uniforms worn by these Royal ladies are exceedingly handsome and very becoming to the beautiful young girls. The eldest, the Grand Duchess Olga, is nearly nineteen years of age. Neither she nor any of her sisters have been named after their Royal great-grandmother, Queen Victoria, as the Rus- sian Church only sanctions national names, or

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Dangers That Lurk in the Gnat Bite.

COLDS NOT DUE TO DRAUGHTS.

Sir Frederick Treves, the famous London physician, astonished the public recently when he said " the idea that colds are caused by draughts is absurd. No cold ever had such an origin. Colds are a result not of draughts, but of stuffy rooms." Other London Physicians back up Treves in holding that no cold ever was caused by a draught.

Cold in the Eyes. The most common disease of the eye is in- flammation of the conjunctiva, and its simplest form is that known as catarrhal ophthalmia. In this the mucous membrane of the eye is affected in the same way as that of the nose and throat, and often occurs at the same time. This disease yields readily to rest and simple treat- ment, but if not checked may run into the puru- lent form of ophthalmia, become chronic, and end in granular lids. The most scrupulous cleanliness must be observed, but a doctor should be consulted at once, as the eye is too delicate an organ to be trifled with, and only a medical man should prescribe anything to be used in it. If the inflamed eyes are bathed in warm water, to which a few drops of laudanum have been added, several times a day, this will usually reduce the inflammation. If the pain is very severe cocaine may give relief, and one to two grains of quinine taken internally will help matters materially.

Gnat Bites. People who live in gnat-infested districts should be very careful how they treat a gnat bite. Gnats may introduce the septic poison- ing into the system, but it is also possible for the person to infect himself by conveying the infection by means of his own nails. Never to scratch a bite would be the proper advice to give, or first of all be sure that the hands are perfectlY clean. Perhaps the best advice would be to recommend everybody to be cautious of approaching garbage or putrefying animal mat- ter when gnats are on the wing. There are many preventives for keeping the gnats at a distance. Oil of lavender, rubbed on the face and hands, is the best of these. To stop the irritation there is nothing better than the old- fashioned housewife remedy, the blue bag, if rubbed immediately on the bite. A little spirits of ammonia is very efficacious, but avoid scratch- ing hard enough to break the skin.

Drowsiness. In general, except when it occurs at the usual bedtime, drowsiness is abnormal, and indicates something wrong either in the body of the sufferer or in his habits. Those who habitually cut off their hours of sleep, the burners of the • midnight oil, pay for their bad habit by at- tacks of sleepiness in the afternoon and early evening. Later, unfortunately, after the influ- ence of digestion wears off, the drowsiness dis- appears, and then, relieved of his burden, the person "sits up to all hours" again, thinking in that way to make up for the hours lost by the drowsiness. If he would abandon his owl- ish habit, go to bed betimes and get the seven or eight hours of continuous sleep that he needs his daytime and evening drowsiness would dis- appear, he could do more and better work, and and life much more enjoyable.

those among their saints for members of the Imperial Family, and Victoria has not yet re- ceived canonisation.

Khaki Weddings. Whilst file war has in some cases postponed a marriage, it has had quite the opposite effect on others, and has hurried on innumerable wed- dings among both rich and poor. One of these hastened weddings was that of the youthful Baroness Beaumont and the Hon. Bernade Howard, heir of Lord Howard of Glossop, which was solemnised at Carlton, Yorkshire, last Sat- urday. Vie event was originally fixed for November, but was thus altered owing to the bridegroom having volunteered for foreign ser- vice with the Lovett Scouts. All over the country registrars and parsons have been kept busy, and the immediate sequel to many of these "war weddings" has been the departure of the husband on active service. This has not been without effect upon Society weddings generally, and there has been a striking change to simpli- city in the fashionable bridal dress; Many brides have dispensed -Oh the train, and the going-away dress is also a much simpler affair. Quiet weddings are everywhere the rule, and a good many brides have preferred to be married in their travelling frocks, and have abandoned 1 the usual social festivities in connection with such an occasion. •

Slump in Fashionable Toilettes. The war has put up the prices of most things, but it has caused something like a slump in all the more expensive autumn toilettes, which in any ordinary season would have attracted the greatest interest just now in Paris and London. In the former capital the most costly dresses were marked down to ridiculous prises, but Frenchwomen hardly know when they will want to wear gay clothes again, and British and American visitors were indifferent to the bar- gains offered in the Rue de la Paix. Hundreds, if not thousands, of these tourists and travellers had abandoned their luggage at hotels and rail- way stations at the more distant resorts in Europe. They will be fortunate if they get their possessions back at some distant date, and meanwhile they refuse to replenish their ward-

robe with anytheing but plain workmanlike suits and simple dresses.

Frenchwomen and the War.

Frenchwomen have nobly responded to the call of Monsieur Poincare to do the work left by the men. Since 1870, women have under- taken many masculine employments, such as lamplighters, dentists, livery stable-keepers, drivers, &c., and if the necessity arose the greater number of these could shoulder guns, and use them well, too. Nearly every French- woman is able to do her husband's work, and not seldom, better than he. The aristocracy of Paris, or rather the feminine portion of it. has, ever since the Franco-Prussian war of 1870, in- terested itself in the work of the Red Cross Society, with the result that there are now hun- dreds of nurses at work with the troops, and all were ready to begin their labours at a few hours' notice. The Government has issued- a Red Cross stamp, and everyone who can afford the extra sou it costs, affixes the little red sq9are to letters sent by post, the profit obtained going to the society.

HOME NOTES AND HINTS. Conserve Food for the Lean Months to Come.

WAR-TI IE RECIPES.

Plums are plentiful this year, but sugar is dear, and an opportunity for laying by a store of jam appears to be neutralised. When sugar can be obtained, however, the jam will not be dearer than usual, as the increase in the cost of sugar is about balanced by the decrease in the cost of the fruit. But sugar in quantities sufficient for jam making purposes on anything like a large scale is difficult to obtain. We can still use our plums to advantage. Plums can be, and should be, preserved by bottling. This can be done without sugar if the bottles containing the fruit and pure water only be stood in a fish kettle or large saucepan up to their necks in water and the water gradually raised to boiling point, the bottles being sealed immediately they are removed. At present hun- dreds of tons of plums in more than one county in England are lying rotting on the ground, or being left ungathered on the trees, owing to a glut in the local market, and the high price of sugar. Bottle them for the winter.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
Preserve Beans.

One of the heaviest garden crops this season is beans. The value of beans in the event of a possible shortage of meat can hardly be over- rated. Beans contain about 25 per cent. of nitrogenous (or flesh-forming) matter, and are, therefore, with the possible exception of fish, eggs and cheese, the best and most easily avail- able substitute for meat. Either the whole or the bulk of the crop should be preserved by placing them in jars, or other open-mouthed re- ceptacles, in layers with salt between them. They could then be used either as a vegetable or as a substitute for meat in the course of next spring without any loss of flavour or nutriment. There is at present a sufficiency of other garden vegetables which render their immediate con- sumption unnecessary. Salt, unlike sugar, has not materially advanced in price.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
Avoid Waste.

- - - - Food left on dishes, no matter what it is, can be used later, but uneaten food left on plates is absolutely wasted. Nothing can be done with it, even by the most economical cook. Each one of us must cease helping himself or herself to more than we intend to finish of sugar, butter, jam, bread, gravy, vegetables, &c. And those who carve must be more careful. It is no good putting on anyone's plate what is unlikely to be eaten. We commonly waste the crust off dry toast, the last, if not the first, crust off many or most of our loaves, a large proportion of the bread that is placed by each person at dinner, all odd pieces cut at table and not wanted at that meal, the crusts off sandwiches and cut or rolled bread and butter. All these, except the broken and crumbled bits on the dinner table, I can be made up again as breadcrumbs, browned or white; as fried bread with soups: bacon or mince; as puddings, savoury or sweet; as stuff- ing; in thick soups, and so forth. But why I have bits. One oz. a day of butter is a very suit- able allowance for a person who has not inueli fat of other kinds, but there is no need for butter with jam or marmalade, or bacon or fried food, or with cheese and biscuits after a good dinner. A crust of dry bread that needs

Fashionable Winter Colours.

Long before there was the slightest inkling of war it was decided by arbiters of modes in the French capital that dark colours were to prevail this autumn and winter. Dark purples, browns, greens, and blues are to be the fashion- able tints, and these are in keeping with the events of the last five or six weeks. Good taste and feeling revolts. from ultra smartness, and the result is that serge tailor-mades, simple in construction and restrained in styles, will be very generally worn. Not only in dark blue, but in stone and other greys, and in all shades of dark green, this material will enjoy a greater vogue than ever before. For smarter occasions, white serge suits are in great demand. Coarse Sicilian alpaca wears quite as well as serge, and is also a favourite just now. The hot weather has brought several new summer goods to the fore, and among them a fabric that has the ap- pearance of a glorified Bolton streeting, most useful for washing suits, and is especially at- tractive in buff and drab shades.

If they are eaten at all, it should be at or immediately after meals in the place of sugar in other forms. Never to eat be- tween meals is a good rule of health. Meat once a day is surely enough for health. With one good meal of meat or fish or game, and an abundance of bread, fruit, and some sort of fat, there is no possible chance of starvation for anybody.—" Queen."

HERE AND THERE.

A man is apt to seta pace in his love letters that he is never able to follow. You can't flatter a homely woman by telling her she is clever unless she isn't. There are about 3,000 weddings every 24 hours, taking the entire world into consideration. The Feud Still On. Ethel: Maud says she is ready to make up if you are. Kate (snappily): Tell her I'd be ready to make up, too, if I had a complexion as muddy as hers. Silk Petticoats Barred Out. . . .

-...„ . Girls have no right to wear silk petticoats in school "because the rustle interferes with the lessons," according to a decision given by the Minister of Education at St. Petersburg. Russia has been charged in the past with almost every form of tyranny and oppression known to man, but this—Ah ! words fail. The girls are up in arms against the decision, but will have to abide by it or leave college. The minister of cult decided the case in favour of several young college professors who claimed the rustling of silk petticoats drew their minds from their work. Light weights are easily moved, whether of brains or feathers, it seems. Terrible News. - - - • - •

Who Said Work? "Haven't you ever thought of going to work?" asked the farmer's wife of Sauntering Sam. " Yes'ra," replied the veteran tramp; "I thought of it once—but I was deeleeryus at de time."

SAUSAGES THAT GROW ON TREES. There are many quaint and interesting things in South Africa, but surely few can rival the "Sausage Tree." This extraordinary tree, which attains a height of about 30 feet, has fruit which resefhbles very large sausages, each mea- suring about 11 feet long. Monkeys love the tree, which is very useful indeed, for the natives eat the fruit, use the wood for making bows and arrows, and the bark for medicinal purposes. Even the leaves are of value, for with the aid of sand they are used for polishing wood.

THE CITY OF MONS.

The deepest coal-working mine in the world is at Mons, in Belgium, which is over 4,000 feet deep. You will all remember that it was near this mining town where our gallant soldiers fought the Germans a few weeks ago.

THE FAIRIES. (BY JESSIE Sum.) There are no fairies, daddy says: And, though he ought to know, I can't agree; because, you see, 'Tis they who make me grow. 'Tie they who send me pleasant dreams When I have gone to rest. They help me, too, in school to do At work, my very best.

Yet, though so kind the fairies are, And make my life so glad, They do not stay, but fly away If my behaviour's bad! 'Tis sad to say that, one dark day, They left me full of woe: But back they came tome again . . . Why doesn't daddy know?

===== 0002601_19140912_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
MY DEAR CHILDREN,— Don't you think it is lucky for you that you live away from the countries where the cruel Germans live? I do. However, you must not stop at thinking how lucky you are, but you must do your share to- wards helping your country to win the war upon which we have entered. You will ask "What can I do?" and say "I am only going to school and they won't let me go to fight." That is true; you cannot go to fight in Ger- many, but you can and must fight at home. The best way in which you can help to beat the Germans is to take notice of all that your teorher says. Superior knowledge will win when the sword has been cast aside. You must re- member that we are not only fighting the Ger- mans on the battlefield, but in the markets of the world. We are trying to take some of their trade away now by force and by energy; but we must beat them by showing the world that we can supply better goods. While at school you should do all you can to learn everything, so that when you com- mence working you will be fit to take your place in the workshop and turn out work that will be better work than the German work. If all our children do their best, 1+ here will Germany be in a few years? I must ask you not to forget the competition announced last week. The subject is "Why I should be loyal "—closing date, September 26th. Your loving UNCLE 'I NED.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
A BRAVE FRENCH BOY.

Now that our soldiers are fighting side by side with the French, it is interesting to recall the brave act of a little French boy over 200 years ago. It was at the time when the Huguenots, who are the Protestants of France, were being very badly treated, because they would not denounce their own religion. In the Village of Thorigne there lived a man, who was a weaver by trade, named Daniel Bonnet; he was a Huguenot, with a wife and three children, whom be wanted to take to America, in order that they would be safe from persecution. He made many preparations to depart. The children had to be placed in bags,. and covered

With vegetables, for their father would have got into serious trouble for taking them away. As the father and mother were leaving the village early one morning, they were terrified to see a soldier coming towards them. He looked suspicious and seemed to know that somebody was hiding in the bags, so he said, in a vary nasty tone: "So you are going to market, are you?" Before they could answer he thrust his sword into the bag. remarking at the same time: "I will try if your carrots are tender." This soldier thought that if there was anybody in the bag he would have squealed, but as no sound came he believed he had made a mistake that time and rode off.

The parents were very much upset, for they knew that one of their little boys was in the bag, yet they did not hear him groan when the soldier used his sword. Then a terrible thought crept into their minds; perhaps he had been killed instantly. However, they dare not look until they could do so in secret, and then they found that the sword had gone through his thigh—yet he had not murmured. The brave little fellow knew that if he had done so his father would have been taken away, and when he looked up he said: "I did not speak, mother," and fell back in a faint. This story of a good son is worth keeping in , mind.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 12, 1914• 4.44444.4.4.40/440.14 4.44.444.4.44444. 4. I FARM AND GARDEN • POULTRY PEN 44 4.• Hints and Interesting Facts and 4. Chat for Amateurs. 014 • 4.444.444.444.4.4.4. 4.4.444,44444.444. There are many people who labour under the delusion that anyone can succeed in the poultry business. As a matter of fact, this is alto- gether wrong, and a comparatively common cause of failure is due to the fact that the person in charge is unsuited to the work. I could tell of a hundred cases during the last three or four years that have come 'under my notioe in which failure has been brought about entirely owing to the incapability of the owner. Many, too, go in for poultry because they have failed at everything else they have tried, or because their health has broken down and they must have an outdoor life. -So far as the latter case is concerned, this is by no means always the cause of failure, for some of. the most suc- cessful men and women I know are those who have been unable to stand a city life. But this is a different thing from being quite broken down and a physical wreck. The numbers who are failures in other walks of life, and who expect to succeed in poultry-keeping is appal- ling, and unless one has actually come into touch with them, one would not believe how many fond parents there are who, because their sons are little better than fools, want them to become poultry-keepers. Poultry-keeping is a stern and serious business, and unless it is regarded so the full measure of success will not be achieved.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
FOXES .AS DISEASE CARRIERS.

Miss Galbraith, who has waged such a plucky, though at times extreme, fight against foxes, contributes a very important letter to a contem- porary, regarding the fox as a carrier of dis- ease. In the course of her remarks, she states: "The Poultry-keepers' Protection Society has received the assurance of one of the chief poultry experts in the country that foxes un- doubtedly carry roup—one of the worst and most contagious diseases of the poultry yard— from farm to farm. Does the country under- stand that there are, roughly, from 200 to 400 foxes roaming free every night in the year over every area of about twenty or thirty miles in which hunting can be carried on, and that foxes are as liable to carry foot-and-mouth disease as dogs are? The farmer is fined if his dog is off the farm after sunset. Why should the fox range free? If the country wishes to control disease—foot-and-mouth as well as others—it must insist upon the confinement of foxes with- in the copses of the hunts—a confinement which in no way interferes with the success of hunt- ing, as has been proved in Hertfordshire. Meantime, some 500,000 uncontrolled foxes roam at large." This is an extremely important, side to the question, and deserves a complete and immediate investigation.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
WEEDING OUT.

The general weakness of the poultry-keeper is to breed more chickens than he should. This is bad enough in itself, but it becomes a much more serious evil when all faulty specimens are allowed to remain, cumbering the run and mon- opolising space in the sleeping-house which ought to contain only the best of their kind. It is often stated that this human weakness of hatching too many chickens had had the direct result of improving the quality of fowls from the standpoint of symmetry and other external signs of beauty, since all chickens are killed over and above those for which the owner has space. If the surplus chickens were disposed of, then doubtless over-hatching would become a virtue; but very seldom are they all sold. Visits to both intensive and extensive breeders reveal the fact that once having got the chick- ens hatched they are indeed loth to part with them for other than profit. They should grasp the fact, however, that destruction in this sense ultimately leads to profit, since it enhances the value of the remainder. Weeding out should be done systematically, and from day to day the chickens should be carefully observed, and as any fault or blemish shows itself they should be ruthlessly dispatched, otherwise the cramped conditions will interfere to a very large extent with the progress of the general flock.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE CAUSES OF DISEASE.

The three chief causes of disease are un- doubtedly negligence in housing, inattention to sanitation, and overstocking the land. There is no end to the evils of bad housing. There are many diseases that owe their beginning to the manner in which fowls are housed, dis- eases which, perhaps, start in a very mild form, but which are often the forerunners of much more serious complaints. Freedom from draughts is an absolute necessity if the inmates are to be kept from colds and the evils that follow in their wake. Badly fitting joints, or boards not closely joined together, or a down- ward current of air, is each quite sufficient to cause disease, and yet they can he so simply remedied with very little trouble and practi- cally no expense. The material of which the house is constructed more often than otherwise is too thin, the wind almost blowing through. In such houses as these the birds are always roosting in a cold atmosphere, which is far from conducive to their well-being. Ventila- tion is of first importance, since this is doubt- less a great „factor towards the maintenance of health. The aim should be to secure adequate ventilation, at the ,same time avoiding a draught. It is equally important that cleanli- ness be strictly observed, for this will assist very considerably in keeping the air sweet. All the internal fittings, such as perches and nest boxes, should be simply constructed and made so that they can easily and quickly be removed. This will ensure the attendant reaching all corners and crevices when cleaning. The perches may rest in slots cut in the framework of the house, and they are thus easily taken out.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPTURE GERMAN TRADE.

Board of Trade Activity.

The volume of inquiry at the Commercial Intelligence Department of the Board of Trade in Basinghall-street for information about op- portunities of capturing German trade shows no diminution. The office has been extended and the staff largely increased. The latter in- cludes a number of experts in particular trades —men who have actually been engaged in these trades and know thoroughly the position in which they stand. The issue of bulletins, two daily, continues steadily, and is in itself a con- siderable work. These bulletins are not, as might be thought from their name, brief state- ments. They run on an average to about 25 printed pages. The subjects of those issued on Thursday were (1) painters' colours and materials; (2) buttons and studs. The bulletins lead to large numbers of inquiries at the bureau for further information. Much help is hoped from the formation of the Sample Exhibition and Catalogue Library. People who cannot exhibit samples may send to the library catalogues showing exactly what goods they want. The Chambers of Commerce have been asked to give their co-operation. The bureau has already issued along DM of articles which people wish to buy or sell. These par- ticulars are being placed in the hands of Cham- bers of Commerce. Further lists will be issued in course of time. The London Chamber of Commerce and other ' bodies are also giving much direct information to business men who have been accustomed to get goods from Germany or other foreign coun- tries and now need to get them elsewhere.

AWFUL ITCHING FROM ECZEMA.

Whitehall Court, Aylesbury, Bucks., Eng.— " I suffered with eczema for eight years. It started on my arms and hands in little spots and pimples and the irritation was unbearable espe- cially at night. It affected me very much in my work, as I had to lose a lot of time owing to my arms and hands being bandaged up. So much irritation was caused that it was a misery to me and it spread very rapidly till it was a mass of running sores. At night the itching was awful; it nearly set me mad. It was very un- sightly; I was ashamed to see people see my hands.

"I tried remedies and 1->ok treatment but to no purpose; they did me lo good at all. So at last I thought I would try a sample of Cuticura Soap and Ointment which I did and found relief after the first application. I had three boxes of Cuticura Soap and Ointment and they effected a complete cure leaving my arms clear of any blemish." (Signed) G. W. Tayler, Jan. 25, 1914. Methods to Meet the Coming Scarcity.

The Board of Agriculture have received the following from the Agricultural Consultative Committee:—

Milk-sellers or others who have a surplus of milk to dispose of are strongly urged to take steps to have it converted into cheese either in their own dairies or co-operatively. This method of dealing with surplus milk beyond what is required for immediate consumption will not only be found more remunerative than separating the milk and making the cream into butter, but will also be a useful means of con- tributing to the conservation of the food supply of the nation,

_ The types of cheeses most suitable for manu- facture under the circumstances are Cheddar, Cheshire, Derby, Leicester, and Gloucester, or such other varieties as do not deteriorate under reasonably prolonged storage.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOD FOR LIVE STOCK.

The Board of Agriculture have given careful consideration to the question of the present and prospective supplies of feeding stuffs for live stock. The large number of cattle in the coun- try renders it necessary to conserve materials required for their maintenance throughout the winter, so as to avoid any excessive rise in the price of meat. The powers given to the Board by the Slaughter of Animals Act, 1914, will, if necessary, be used to prevent the wasteful deple- tion of the home meat supply, but it is import- ant to ensure so far as possible that the cost of feeding stuffs shall be kept at a reasonable level. With this object the exportation of feeding stuffs for animals is generally prohibited, but the Board are prepared to consider applications for licence to export small quantities of certain feeding stuffs, so long only as the prices at which they can be obtained by farmers in this country are fair and moderate.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
RISKS OF EUROPEAN SCARCITY.

The outlook justifies the prediction of a scarcity of European wheat in 1915, says "The Times." Many of the chief centres of produc- tion are so seriously devastated and the working forces so sadly disorganised that it would seem vain to hope that the normal production can be maintained.

The relief supplies must come in bulk from the great corn-producing countries of North and South America, Australasia, and India. As far as our own Dominions and Colonies are con- cerned, we can rely upon the double incentive of sentiment and the prospect of a good market to augment the areas of corn to the fullest..ex- tent.

Various estimates have been expressed as to the extent of the British contribution. The country has to raise more stock as well as more wheat, and the growing of wheat is less com- patible than any other agricultural pursuit with a full complement of cattle and sheep, though possibly not of pigs. It has been suggested that wheat might supersede barley, since the latter is used chiefly in the production of articles not essential in times of anxiety. A large propor- tion of the barley crop—too large from the growers' point of view—is ground as a food for stock, but there are physical as well as climatic obstacles to the adoption of this plan. It has also been suggested that the yield of wheat could be increased by the liberal use of manures. The Consultative Committee to the Board of Agriculture can be trusted to give sound advice upon matters of this kind. The problem before breeders of plants is to raise a breed of wheat that will withstand the rains and storms in July. It has been found an easier task to pro- pagate heavy reproduction of grain than to evolve a variety strong enough in the straw to carry the head to full maturity. A Wasteful Dairying System.

The position as regards live stock is satisfac- tory. The statement of the Board of Agricul- ture that the number of cattle in the United Kingdom is larger than in any previous year is interesting in view of the suggestion that breed- ing stock were being rushed on the market in response to the rise in prices. There is one matter relating to the supply of cattle that merits immediate attention. Thou- sands of the best dual purpose cows are sacri- ficed annually to the convenience of a system of specialised dairying that is of advantage only to those who practise it. The finest shorthorn and cross-bred cows from Westmorland, Cum- berland, and elsewhere are bought in soon after calving, milked, and fattened off in their prime. The method is profitable, no doubt, but it is very wasteful of a valuable source of national wealth. The authorities might well step in and prevent this severe drain upon the supply of the most serviceable cows the country possesses.

IN THE GARDEN.

Vegetables That May Be Planted Now.

.__ ......... The following vegetables may be planted in early Septern.ber:—

1, early cabbage, any early variety; 2, broc- coli, winter and spring varieties; 3, borecole or kale, winter and spring varieties; 4, leeks, Mus- selburgh.

_ They- should be planted in the following way. viz.:—

I. Early cabbage.—Plant in -rows, leaving 15in. between the plants and 18in. between the rows. Very dwarf varieties, however, succeed if only 12in. are left between the plants, and 15in. between the rows. Strong growing late varieties require 18in. between the plants and 271 n. between the rows. The plants must be earthed up with a plough or hoe as the crop grows.

2. Broccoli.—Plant in rows, leaving 18in. be- tween the plants and 27in. between the rows. The crop should he earthed up as in the case of cabbage.

_ 3. Borecole or kale.—Plant in rows the same as broccoli.

4. Leeks.—Plant in rows, leaving 4in. to 6in. between the plants and 12in. to 15in. between the rows. CROPPING WASTE LANDS.

The Rev. W. Wilks, secretary of the R.H.S., and Prof. F. W. Keeble, director of the R.H.S. Gardens at Wisley, have made a timely sugges- tion that the multitude of small unused or un- cropped plots of ground should be sown with such food plants as it is possible to sow at this period of the year. At Slough undeveloped building sites have been offered rent free to those who will undertake to use them for grow- ing crops to increase the food supply. There is certainly room for the extension of the admir. able work accomplished by the Vacant Land Cultivation Society, for which the late Mr. Joseph Fels laboured so earnestly. The Church Army has also done good service in Westmin- ster, Walworth, and elsewhere in making use of the unemployed in cultivating land which awaits the builder's attention. Creditable specimens of the vegetables groVn—orilhese plots have been exhibited at the Royal Horticultural Society's shows. A full utilisation of. our own resources is one of the necessary provisions for future contingencies.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

CUT FLOWERS IN ROOMS. A great many of the different outdoor flowers that are at their best in September are adapted for the embellishment of the dwelling-house when cut, says the "Agricultural Economist." Asters, lieleniums, helianthus, and others may be cut in the form of long sprays that readily lend themselves to furnishing large vases. They admit of a lighter arrangement than is possible with short-stemmed flowers, while if gathered at a suitable time they last well. The Michael- mas daisies and similar kinds should be gath- ered just as the earliest flowers are opened. By changing the water frequently and cutting off any spent blossoms the same sprays will keep up a display for some time. Complaints are some.. times made that dahlias do not last long when cut, which is, to a certain extent, correct. Still, they well repay a little care and attention, espe- cially the single and collarette varieties, which are more appreciated as cut flowers than the other kinds. In the first place the blooms should be gathered early in the morning, before they have felt the influence of the sun, and at once put into water. They should be freshly expanded.

THE CYCLISTS' NOTEBOOK.

PLACES WORTH SEEING.

The Devil's Bridge.—There is, perhaps, no prettier stretch of river scenery in this country than may be found on the Lune, at Kirkby Lonsdale, that quaint old market town in West- morland, an easy week-end ride from Warring- ton, namely, 67 miles, via Lancaster and Hornby. The river is spanned by an elegant bridge, which, the legend says, was constructed by his Satanic majesty. The cow of a poor woman had strayed across the river at low water, and she was at her wit's end how to recover it. At this juncture the King of Evil made his appearance, and agreed to build a bridge so that she might reach her cow, on condition that the first living thing which crossed it should be his, and so the structure was raised. Her husband had gone' that day to market, and she expected his return every minute. Satan knew this, and hoped to capture him, hut directly his wife spied him coming down the opposite bank she sent her dog across the bridge to meet him, exclaiming: "Now, crafty sir, the bargain was, That you should have what first did pass Across the bridge: so now, alas! The dog's your right. The cheater cheated, struck with shame, Squinted and grinned, then in a flame He vanished quite." ADAM'S GOOD FORTUNE. Old Adam was a lucky man, Although We know He thought his lot Was very hard. He once was caught clean off his guard, With consequences that were fraught With lasting woe. Old Adam was a lucky man, For he Was not compelled to stay And see When Eve, his wifey, rode away Like the wind, "If my skirts are hanging straight, And do I seem to look all right From behind?" BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

SATURDAY.

Before Mr. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair),

Alderman Tinnion, Mr. T. Pemberton and

Mr. A. J. Jolley. A Pair of Trousers.—Florrip Annie Crombie, charwoman, giving the address, 89, Bewsey- street, was charged with stealing on September 2nd a pair of trousers, value 35., from the shop 55, Bewsey-street.—Supt. Williamson said it was necessary to ask for a week's remand, but if they completed their inquiries before that time the prisoner would be dealt with sooner. On the previous Wednesday Crombie went to the shop of Mr. John Grocott, in Bewsey-street, and asked for a pair of second-hand trousers, but as there were none in the shop she was shown a new pair, valued at 3s. She asked permission to take them away to see "if they would suit," and the shop- keeper sent a nine-year-old boy with her. How- ever, she decamped and the property was re- covered the previous day from a house prisoner had visited in Gladstone-street.—The remand was granted.—The prisoner was again brought up on Thursday morning and committed to prison for one month. --

The Lodger's Early Rise.--Ilarry Bennett (17), fi, Torkington Court, Brinksway, Stockport, was charged with stealing on August 26th from the dwelling-house, 23, Myrtle-grove, Warrington, one overcoat, two jackets, one vest, one cap and one muffler, value in all 308., the property of William Nixon and others.—Supt. Williamson asked for a week's remand. The boy had been lodging with the Nixons and early on the morn- ing of the day in question he got up early to go to work and later, when one of the family went downstairs, he observed that the articles had been taken. Bennett's description was circu- lated, with the result that he was arrested in Stockport the previous day.—The remand was granted.

HELPING THE HAPLESS.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
White Heather _ Day Proceeds

Divided.

TWO LOCAL CHARITIES BENEFIT.

One of the odd beliefs which find currency even among those who would strongly resent being described as superstitious endows the bloom of the white heather bush with the power to bring good fortune. Certain it is that sprigs of the pretty moorland plant have been the means of bringing a happy seaside holiday to many a poor Warrington child and sorely-needed comforts to the bedside of not a few of the im- poverished sick people of the town. It came about in this way: Two of the local bowling associations—the Caledonian and the Works Association—cast about them to see what they could do for "sweet charity's sake," and, in addition to playing an exhibition match, at which a charge for admission was made, they hit upon the happy idea of selling sprigs of "the bonny bloom" in the streets, the money raised by game and sale, less a small sum for expenses, to go in half shares to the Warring- ton Children's Summer Camp Fund and the Warrington Sick Nursing Association. Match and sale combined realised a net profit of £3O, and, at a gathering of members of the two bowl- ing clubs held at the Lion Hotel on Tuesday evening, cheques for £l5 each were formally handed over to representatives of the two-Taise- worthy bodies named. The Mayor was to have had the pleasure of presenting the cheques, but at the last moment he found himself unable to be present.

A MATTER FOR SELF-CONGRATULATION.

Mr. J. L. Davenport occupied the chair, and, in opening the proceedings, said he thought they might congratulate themselves upon the success which had attended their efforts to help two of the most deserving of the local charities. (Hear, hear.) The object they had in hand was one which must appeal to every man, whatever his position in life, viz., the helping of those who were in the unfortunate position of not being able to help themselves. By establishing what had become known locally as White Heather Day, they had not only succeeded in interest- ing the -bowling fraternity in their act of charity, but had also made a direct and success- ful appeal to the whole town. Mr. Tennant, of the Caledonian Club, then presented a brief financial statement, which showed receipts totalling .635 15s. 9d.—including £l7 10s. 6d. collected in the boxes—and expenses amounting to -65 15s. 9d., thus leaving a balance of £3O to be divided among the two bodies to be benefited.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS WHERE THANKS ARE DUE.

Mr. Patrick, secretary of the Works Associa- tion, said he was sure all concerned were de- lighted with the amount raised, though they hoped to improve on it on future occasions. He then proceeded to propose an omnibus vote of thanks to Messrs. Eustance for giving a watch to be drawn for—a fact which, he thought, in- fluenced the sale of tickets very considerably— to the lady sellers and helpers, amongst whom he would specially mention Mrs. Tennant; and to Mr. Earle for kindly placing at their dis- posal the Brooklands bowling green in Lovely- lane.

Mr. Burdett, in seconding, said he was sure he was voicing the feelings of all present when he said that he hoped that the good work would proceed for many years to come, and that suc- cess, even greater than that achieved this year, would attend the efforts put forth. The proposition was carried with applause. and the Chairman, acknowledging it on behalf of his firm (Messrs. Eustance), said that if on any future occasion he, and those he repre- sented, could be of any assistance, he did not think the promoters of White Heather Day would require to make a second application.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME SUGGESTIONS.

The Chairman ,then called for suggestions as to future efforts of a like character. Mr. Patrick offered the first, which was to the effect that next time the objects of the howling match and heather sale should be most clearly stated, instead of it being left at the inexplicit phrase "for local charities." They could not have two finer objects to work for than the Nursing Association and the Summer Camp, and they should give the public clearly to under- stand what they were asking them to help, viz., two organisations that had to do with the poorest of the poor. He thought they should also make a request to the Mayor to be allowed the use of a room in one of the public buildings in which to break up the white heather and, generally, use as headquarters for the sellers, &c. In pursuance of the second suggestion Coun- cillor Jenkins and Messrs. Patrick and Tennant were appointed a committee to interview the Mayor with a view to obtaining the use of a room in the Gymnasium.

PRESENTATION OF CHEQUES. The Chairman then formally presented cheques for .#l5 to Messrs. J. H. Ransome and George Walker, as representing, respectively, the Children's Camp and the Nursing Society. In returning thanks, Mr. Ransome said that this year the camp at Prestatyn, North Wales, was only open three weeks, owing to the rail- way company being compelled to withdraw cheap travelling facilities, consequent upon the war. During the three weeks 400 poor children were taken to the seaside for a holiday. The cost was about ss. Bd. per head per week, includ- ing railway fare. Children who should have gone to the camp for the fourth week, but could not be taken owing to the war, were given a treat at Frodsham instead. The society he had the honour to represent had offered the camp to the Government as a suitable place for convales- cent soldiers.

Thanking the company on behalf of the Nurs- ing Association, Mr. Walker said that the nurses had not only done a great service to the poorer among the community by means of actual nursing, but they had also been able to give a lot of good and useful advice, which was much appreciated by the recipients._

The proceedings terminated with a vote of thanks to the Chairman, proposed by Councillor Jenkins and seconded by Mr. McDermid.

A Good-natured King. King Edward's good nature was illustrated the other night by a London correspondent at the Press Club in New York.

"The King," said the correspondent, "was visiting Ruiford Abbey, and one morning, in company with his host, Lord Arthur Savile, he took a walk in the preserves. 'Suddenly Lord Arthur, a big, burly man, rushed forward and seized a shabby fellow with a dead pheasant protruding from the breast of his coat.

" ' Sir,' said Lord Arthur to the King, 'this fellow is a bad egg. This is the second time I've caught him poaching.' "But the King's handsome face beamed, and he laughed his gay and tolerant laugh. "'Oh, let him go,' he said. 'lf he really were a bad egg, you know he wouldn't poach.'"

Growing Cost of War. In the first days of the war of 1870 France opened a credit of £22,000,000 and Germany one of £20,000,000; and "The Economist" of the last week of July, 1870, writes of these sums as being on "an immense scale." For the present war the Reichstag began with a. vote for £250,000,000 and the House of Commons -400,000,000.

HUSBAND AND WIFE "ON BUSINESS LINES."

A Liberal claimant for an occupier's vote told the Barrister that he lived in a house belonging to his wife, to whom he paid a monthly equiva- lent to the rent, in addition to housekeeping money, and in proof of his statement he pro- duced two rent books, explaining, "The wife and myself do it on business lines." Asked why he had not claimed before, the claimant said that he had been thinking of doing so, but had put it off because he under- stood that the Government were likely to bring in a measure which would have entitled him to a vote without the necessity of going through the present procedure. "However," he added, " when I saw the portion which would have en- titled me cut out of the Bill. I claimed." After scrutinising the rent books very care- fully, the Barrister observed: "Personally, I have very great doubts, but you have been ' sworn and are liable to penalties if you have not spoken the truth. I think I must accept what you say, and the vote will be allowed."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS TO THE BARRISTER.

The Court sat again on Wednesday morning, when the business was of a routine character, and quite devoid of any points of public inter- est.

On Thursday morning the Barrister put the final touches to his task and before he quitted the Town Hall he was accorded the customary vote of thanks. _ _

In proposing it, Mr. Hayes said that this year the work had been exceedingly easy; in fact, it seemed to get easier every year. There had been no objections, thanks to an arrangement between the parties, which, under the circumstances, was a very good thing. In this connection he might add that a compact had been made whereby his party and the Liberals had Funk their politics for the time being. In view of the present crisis both parties stood united in their willingness to assist the Government at present in power to see things through. He hoped that next year's court would be held under happier circum- stances.

In seconding the proposition, Mr. Sanders said he would like to endorse what Mr. Hayes had said. Under ordinary circumstances the new register would have been an important one, as a General Election would have been fought on it. But now, of course, the election had been deferred for a twelvemonth, anti- as the new register was not of that degree of import- ance that it might have been to the respective political parties, a compact had been made, as Mr. Hayes had stated, between the Liberals and the Tories, by which the work of revising the voters' list had this year been made compara- tively easy.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
WILL THE LABOURITES COME INTO LINE?

It is just possible, continued Mr. Sanders, that the Labour party in Warrington might be induced to come into line, having regard to the present tragic circumstances. There was no doubt that there was a strong feeling in the Labour ranks that political fights should be at any rate postponed for a further twelve- months. At one time it appeared probable that the present Government would introduce legis- lation to abolish Revision Courts, and he had begun to think that there was a possibility that they would not have had the Barrister with them after another twelve months, but, person- ally, he was pleased to say that it was now just possible that Mr. Wharton might be with them for many years to come.

A BARRISTER AND ALIENS. In acknowledging the compliment, the Bar- rister said he always regarded that vote of thanks as a very important matter, because he felt that it was something of a feat to be abso- lutely independent and yet gain the regard of both sides. He tried hard to be perfectly fair. However, he could not quite agree with every. thing that was said about ignoring politics at the present juncture. Certainly let them ignore politics so far as their own feelings were con- cerned, and among themselves, but at the same time, whilst getting everything done without any heat, they must not forget that the object of the Revision Court was to have a proper regis- ter drawn up. He would not like it to be thought that because a war was going on the duties of the Revising Barrister, or of those who assisted him, must be in any way neglected. In this instance he felt quite certain that Mr. Woodcock would keep everybody in order, and that the list would be what lie might call a pure one. He thought that, although the agents had not fought each other vigorously, they had, nevertheless, done their duty to their respective parties. One thing that had been talked about a great deal, the Barrister continued, was the question of aliens. Personally, he thought it was a case of the less said in those courts on that subject the better it would be. Certain proceedings were being constantly taken by the police, and personally he felt that in those courts they ought to keep perfectly quiet on the matter and let the police do their duty without being hampered by any statements or disclosures made to Revis- ing Barristers. He found that in several places the lists of objections and claims were larger than last year, so that it was net every town that was acting as Warrington had acted. He did not think it was necessary that this town should act as others he had in mind were act- ing, because he was perfectly satisfied that the system adopted by Mr. Woodcock and his staff for keeping the register in order was quite cor- rect. Mr. Woodcock was so correct in the man- ner in which he carried out his duties that there was really very little for him (the Barrister) to do. They owed a great deal to Mr. Woodcock. In conclusion Mr. Wharton expressed the hope that they would all meet again this time next year and under more auspicious circumstances. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140912_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
READING AND TRADE.

In this time of crisis we call it our duty to I stand by our fellow-peogle, to help those that suffer, and, above all, to do nothing that will aggravate unemployment—to keep, in fact, " businesa as usual." But people do not realise the intertelation of trades or how the least laxity from normal occupations can affect a thousand, or sometimes ten thousand, work- people employed in the production of an article that we decide to "do without." Take, for instance, the simple occupation of reading. The employment starts with the author who in normal times hands over his manuscript to a publisher employing perhaps 20 men in his own office. The publisher finds work for printers, paper-makers, engravers, artists, designers, ink- makers, card-makers, cloth-makers, advertising agents, distributors, booksellers, librarians, and indirectly for glue-makers, tape-makers, thread- makers, and a hundred other trades; a well- known paper manufacturer pays out over,C6,ooo weekly in wages, another firm £2,000, and so on. But all these trades are dislocated utterly, because novels—the best possible source of dis- traction and peace—are left alone without a thought of the effect of their neglect. It is earnestly to be hoped that every man or woman whose custom it is to read novels or more serious books will realise the absolute duty of continuing to buy or borrow them, particularly those being prepared and issued during the pre- sent time, and thus keep in employment the workers of this trade.

At the Town Hall on Tuesday morning Mr. C. H. Marriott Wharton, Revising Barrister, commenced the annual task of amending and bringing up to date the list of electors for the Parliamentary Division of the borough of War- rington. In this matter he had the help of Mr. W. H. Woodcock (assistant overseer), whilst the interests of the two big political parties were looked after by Messrs. E. A. Sanders (Liberal agent) and Herbert Hayes (Conserva- tive agent). The proceedings were divested of a measure of their usual burning partisan character by the fact that in view of the war an understanding had been arrived at between the political agents, under which neither of them contested the claims put forward by the other. At the outset the Barrister dealt with the claims for lodger votes.

CONVENIENCE DOES NOT OUTWEIGH

VALUE.

The first claimant on the lodger list, Mr. Charles Sidney Cooper, appeared in person. His claim was supported by Mr. Sanders, who ad- mitted that the house in which the claimant resided was under value, but submitted, as a set-off, that, for the purposes of the claimant himself the house had "a very, very good value," inasmuch as it was little more than a stone-throw from his place of employment, viz., Messrs. Crosfield's works, in which he was a draughtsman. The house was rented by Mrs. McLaren, who had two sons and two daughters residing with her, in addition to the claimant, who had a bedroom and sitting-room to himself. Claimant interpolated the information that there was a second lodger in the house, occupy- ing a separate room, and, later on, added that Mrs. McLaren had three sons living with her, one being a little boy. Mr. Sanders submitted that this was one of those cases in which regard should be paid to the fact that the place where the claimant re- sided was very convenient to him, and there- fore was of special value to him. It was a matter of value, pure and simple, to the lodger. It having been pointed out that the house was assessed at only ..tl9 10s. Mr. Sanders remarked that he was not pleading for the vote on a value of £lO, and repeated that he was asking that the matter of absolute convenience to the Claimant should be taken into consideration., It was true that the claimant could go else- where, but it was a difficult matter to get lodg- ing adjacent to Crosfield's works. The Revising Barrister: You are asking me to depart from my usual custom. Mr. Sanders: It is a serious matter to him, as far as value is concerned. He cannot get lodgings elsewhere conveniently close to his work.

Mr. Hayes submitted that, as had been decided several times in that Court, the matter of con- venience did not give value. Mr. Sanders: It does make a big difference. Mr. Hayes: The only question is that the house is near the mau's place of employment, and I say that is of no value. Mr. Sanders: I maintain that it is.

~,,, ..... The Revising Barrister: It cuts both ways: He ought to be able to get much better rooms than these.

Mr. Sanders: Not in the vicinity. The Assistant Overseer: Including rooms, furniture and attendance, he only pays 7s. a week. That, I take it, is indicative of the poor quality of the room. He ought to be paying 10s. or 12s. a week.

Mr. Sanders : Notwithstanding, I hope the Barrister will favour the claimant and will give him the benefit of the doubt.

The Revising Barrister: No, I don't think there is value.

Mr. Sanders: There is no Parliamentary vote attached to the house. Might that fact help a little bit?

The Barrister shook his head in token of dissent.

LAPSE OF TIME REMOVES AN OBSTACLE.

In the matter of the claim of Mr. Sidney Wills Maskery Davey, of 5, Grappenhall-road, Mr. Sanders said that last year the claimant applied, but was refused a vote on the ground that a lady and gentleman lodged in the house in addition to himself. This time that objection did not hold good, as there were no other lodgers in the house, which was of good value, and contained five bedrooms. The claimant was the son of a widow, and was practically the up-keeper of the whole house. Remarking that he thought the value was all right, and that he had no reason to suppose that there was anything unproper about the claim, the Barrister allowed the vote.

T)-(E CYURCHES AND THE WAR.

hard to give them the place in the policy cf nations which might had formerly held. Above all the exhibitions of force, strength, cunning, courage and bravery there now stood the greater Christian ideals of God's Father- hood, man's brotherhood, and the world-wide Divine kingdom—these they must regard as the only right, and in spite of failure and disap- pointment, in spite of death and destruction, even in the face of the present desperate situa- tion, they must not lose their faith, but strive on, as men not without hope, who could see the dawn of a fairer morrow.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEATH-BED OF NATIONS And the Birth-pangs of a New Age.

The forty-first anniversary of the dedication of Wycliffe Congregational Church was cele- brated on Sunday, when the Rev. T. T. James conducted the services. In the morning the preacher spoke upon " The Claims of the Church," taking for his text I. Corinthians xii. 27, "Now ye are the body of Christ." This, he said, was the logical outcome of Paul's thought. Christ was once in the flesh; men could look at Him, and speak with Him. Now, said Paul, He was in the flesh no more, and made the startling statement, " Ye (the church) are the body of Christ." The church was the visible manifestation of His presence, and upon it men could set their eyes, to see what Christ was like, though His outward, physical body had passed away. But no official authority could make a church. The seal of the Congregational Union was as void and ineffective as the blessing of an archbishop, unless the blessing of Christ rested upon it. In the evening Mr. James spoke upon "The Hour of Crisis," taking for his text, "God is our refuge and strength," Psalm xlvi. This day was the anniversary of the church, and they must all be met there in the spirit of gratitude for the divine goodness bestowed upon them through the years. He thought it well in their worship that night that their prayer and thought should gather round the national and world crisis, from which it was impossible for their minds to escape. Difficult enough it was to preserve calm of spirit, because of the grim uncertainty of the moment. Their thoughts were with those millions of men ranged in con- flict on the plains of Northern France and around the fair city of Paris. Unless we were much mistaken, we were looking on at the death bed of nations, and certainly we were witnessing the birth pangs of a new age. The very dis- tance from the scene, a blessing to this land in one way, was yet a burden in another, for it was weary work waiting for news that might settle the destinies of peoples, and give a new turn to the history of the world. From spiritual sources they needed to feed their faith and courage in this hour of crisis. The only fortitude which lasted was that which was built upon religious faith.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
MIGHT AND RIGHT.

Rev. Irvine Lister Commences Ills Ministry at Cairo-street Chapel.

The Unitarian congregation who worship in the Cairo-street Chapel welcomed their new pastor on Sunday by well-attended services. The Rev. Irvine Lister, who comes from West Dids- bury, Manchester, succeeds the Rev. Stanley Mellor, who severed his connection with War- rington several months ago to undertake more important work for the denomination in Liver- pool. On Sunday morning the new pastor, preaching upon the subject " Might and right," said they were all vastly impressed by the fact that they were living in a time of crisis such as the world had never known before, and such as it was not likely to know again. This had been empha- sised on every hand, and there was no doubt that, as a people, they had been, and were being, more moved than they could well express. They heard around them the clash of arms and the booming of guns, and in imagination they heard the cries of the wounded, the homeless and sufferers.

Civilisation Cast Into Melting Pot. They were well aware that tremendous issues were going forward, and though they had confi- dence and hope, they knew not the end. Nation had risen against nation, ideals had been shat- tered, and the whole of the boasted Western civilisation had been cast into the melting pot. They trusted that right would triumph, and that eventually they would gain once more a state of peace, but which would have a surer foundation, and remain for ever. In this time of crisis they did not care very much to talk of moral and religious ideals, for they were mocked by the tramp of armed men and seemed to have no place or power in their present state. With the demolition of towns and the shattering of forts had come the shat- tering of many men's beliefs—the goodness of human nature and even in God; one was almost as terrible to contemplate as the other. Such a violent upheaval had far-reaching effects, and not the least of these were the effects produced on one's cherished beliefs.

" Easy to Scoff at Religion." It was easy now for the scoffer to scoff at religion, to hold up the simple beauty of Jesus Christ to ridicule, and point the finger of scorn at the churches, and, although perhaps men would not be so cruel as to do that, they must be ready. Even as their civilisation was in danger, KO was their religion, and it was because he felt it so strongly that he suggested a simple consideration of the subject of might and right. Their moral ideals if not swept away altogether were likely to be vastly changed, and at the end they might, if they were not careful, find them- selves with a new set of principles not so fine and noble as the ones they now possessed; prin- ciples which would make the world poorer. Therefore they must endeavour to keep their ideals high and their aspirations pure, because they had a duty not only to themselves but to those who would come after them.

Might's Great Part. Anyone who was capable of taking a compre- hensive survey of the history of men or of human society from the time when man first realised he was man until the present moment, could not but be impressed by the fact that might—brute strength, physical energy, force— had played a very great and significant part in his development. The early life of man pre- sented to them almost as much struggling for survival as did the life of plants and animals, for only by cunning did premature man ward off the attacks of wild beasts, only by sheer physical strength did he tame Nature and make her subservient to his ends, only by fighting did he protect his family and tribe from the maraudering expeditions of other families and tribes, and only by constant watchfulness and persistent warfare did he win his right to live. But it seemed to the student of history that even after men had passed.from the tribal stage the greatest factor of his development was still might. To take by force of arms whatever they could was justice, and that recognised law neces- sitated the maintenance of a superior fighting strength, for always the weakest went to the wall and were destroyed. Might was right, and those who had not might on their side had there- fore no right of protection or even of life.

Greater Rights Than Might. Had there been any change during the last 20 centuries? Had not the great nations come into being by means of conquest, by warlike ex- pansion of their territory, by subjugating smaller nations and absorbing them, by might and power? Might seemed to have been an es- sential factor in the evolution of men and nations—and without it it would be difficult to understand how development would have been possible at aIL The use of force in the past had not altogether been an evil, and realising the position as it stood at present they were bound to admit that great prosperity, some happiness. much of the fulness and abundance of life had been won by means of might. But they were compelled to ask the question "Has might always been right, and will might in the future be right?" It had not always been right in the past, neither could it be maintained mat it would be right in the future, for now there were other and greater matters to consider. Surely they were now in such a state of develop- ment that they had passed beyond the need of might for further development. In these days they had to realise that there was a greater right than might, and that by the greater right their future development would proceed.

Highest Interests Served by Peace. For centuries now men bad contemplated Chris- tian ideals—God's Fatherhood, the sonship and brotherhood of man—and they had realised, too, that all the highest interests of men were. best served by peaceful co-operation one with an- other, by sympathy, by love, by obedience to truth, and they had come to the conclusion that those Christian ideals were a right, greater than all the might of the past. Those great religious ideals had been exalted and men had striven

fr,e;e4,44,1Z.

TIIE RECTOR ON EYE-OPENING.

Praise for the Government.

The Rector <the I?,ev. Canon Willis), preaching at ,the Warrington Parish Church on Sunday evening, referred to the many points upon which the Germans' eyes had been opened since the outbreak of war, as the eyes of the enemies of Israel were opened to the greater force that was on their side than had been imagined. Among other things the Germans reckoned an our Army being small and negligible, a point upon which they had been, and would be, enlightened. They also thought the two political parties in this country were bitterly hostile and that the Orange party and Roman Catholics were on the point of creating a revolution in Ireland. All differences were sunk the moment war was de- clared. The Germans thought we would not fight for what they called "a scrap of paper," but that scrap of paper contained oar word of honour to safeguard Belgium and befriend France.

On our side the war had taught us that there was good on both sides in Parliament. He had been brought up a strong Conservative and Unionist, but he had not previouily realised how much good there was on the other side in politics. Nobody could read, the magnificent speeches of the Prime Minister, Sir 'Edward Grey, and Mr. Redmond, without feeling that there was good on both sides. Our eyes had also been opened as to the real object of the German army and navy. A while back he read some striking articles on the intentions and prepara- tions of Germany by Mr. Robert Blatchford. People laughed at them in those days, and ridi- culed the idea that the German forces were not for the purposes of defence and protection, but for invasion and destruction.

Englishmen must now appreciate the necessity for a strong army and navy. We had a splen- did navy, but our army was very small. If we had only listened to the warnings of Lord Roberts, who told us over and over again how unwise we were not to have more adequate mili- tary forces for the protection of our possessions, we should have been in a very different position. He held that when the time for settlement. came there should be no reprisals, but that we should insist on an ample indemnity to cover the destruction that had been wrought in Bel- gium and elsewhere, and that there should be a disarmament of the German fleet that had been created not to protect German commerce, hut to crush European liberty under a system that pretended that might was right.

FRIENDLY SOCIETY WORK.

Mayor on Its Vaks as Training.

On Saturday night the Worthy Chief of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (Bro. Robin- son Wardman, of Leeds) paid an official visit to the members of the Order in Warrington. A large company of the local brethren assembled at the Central Hotel to give him a welcome. The Mayor (Alderman Peacock) presided, and supporting him were Councillor J. T. Barton, and Bros. George Rogers, P.M.; Win. Eckersley, Lawrence Weir, Joe. Adams, J. Haley, Thoe. Baskerville, Thos. Miller, and others. After ample justice had been done to an ex- cellent spread, provided by Host Roberts, the usual loyal toast was enthusiastically honoured. The Mayor spoke in felicitous terms of the interest he took in Friendly Society work, and of the great and noble human qualities it brought out in those who put enthusiasm into their efforts on its behalf. He attributed a large measure of his success in life to the training he got from Friendly Society work, and urged others to take up this sphere of usefulness in the development of their character. The ad- dress, which was well delivered, was very warmly received.

Bro. Haley, who welcomed the Chief on behalf of the local members, said he did so as a fellow Yorkshireman. He hoped that during his term of office Bro. Wardman would display that ster- ling grit so characteristic of his fellow county men.

Bro. Wardman, who was greeted with loud applause, spoke of the great responsibihty rest- ing upon all who were engaged in Friendly Society work, particularly at this critical zit.e.ed in the history of our country. Dwelling yi.en the State Insurance Scheme, he expressed the opinion that the long training of tlfe Friendly Society men well fitted them to administer a scheme of such magnitude and complexity, bringing, as they would, the qualities of economy and watchfulness into their work. Bro. George Rogers, in happy terms, pro: en-d the toast of "The Mayor," which was received with musical honours.

In reply, his Worship observed that lie 'vas always happy to be among ',lendly Sviety men, who, he knew, were sta,m^li friends of his. Mr. William Whittle entertained the com- pany with a number of musical items, nrd Mr. Harry Woodall Townley (of Chorley) contri- buted a good song.

WHAT THE GERMAN EMPEROR OVERLOOKED.

We do not desire to be prematurely optimistic or to be congratulatory before we are quite out of the wood, but the reports from the war last night justify us in assuming a satisfied air in the expectation of further favours to come. The Germans swept down

almost to the gates of Paris, but they • were destined not to look upon the fair city while on this excursion. Their hordes were forced to turn aside, and now they are retracing their steps at a greater rate even than their anxiety to reach the capital of France imposed upon them a few days ago. It will be a saddening thought to the HAITI that he got so near to his coveted prize and is now solar removed from it. But His IMPERIAL MAJESTY forgot one or two things, if he ever knew anything about them, which he would have done well to remember. It will be seen that SIR JOHN FRENCH attributes this startling collapse before the onslaught of the British troops to demoralisation, brought about by drunkenness and the atrocities of various kinds that the Ger- mans committed during their advance. They now find themselves returning through a devastated country that gives them neither comfort of body nor peace of mind. It is the old truth that the wages of sin is death. The KAISER overlooked that little fundamental fact of human life, and he is sufferinz the penalties, and will do so, of his culpabLa ignorance. These are the passive but inexorable penalties of his lack of knowledge of human affairs and human laws. The active penalties are as powerful and will contribute as surely to his undoing. It is the cruel manner in which his war has been waged that has stirred the manhood of the British Empire to send against him its hun- dreds of thousands of voluntary war- riors that will sweep his drunken legions of conscripts from their paths

and annihilate for ever that Kaiserdom that has so wantonly flung a happy and a prosperous world into misery and ruin. India will send its 70,000 men

and its princes to fight the Mad Man

of Europe, who thought, as madmen do think, that they were mad and would take the chance to break With English rule, The Boers of South Africa are as anxious to stand side by side with us against this magnified brigand as are the ultra- loyalist of Canada. The whole of the Empire, the whole of the world, con- spires for the overthrow of the monster who was so debased as to think that Twentieth Century civilisation was brought about for the purpose of promiscuously cutting one another's throats.
