PASSING WEEK,

DOING OUR DUTY.

Warrington-is doing its duty along with the rest of the country. We have sent forth our sons in such numbers that the pinch has been felt at several of the works, especially those engaged on Government work for the war. We have apparently reached a point of equilibrium at which the value of the man at home equals the value of the man at the front, because the necessaries of the war are as important as the man who uses the gun. Owing to the large amount of war material wanted from Warring- ton there is no unemployment. When we have captured the German iron trade we shall be in a very strong position locally.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
SECOND BATCH OF TERRITORIALS.

Monday saw the departure of the second batch of Territorials who had volunteered for foreign service. They numbered 300 and were intended to bring up our local battalion to full strength when it goes abroad. The 300 "Terriers" who did not volunteer for foreign service will be the nucleus of a reserve Terri- torial Battalion that is to be raised to take the place of those abroad.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE NOVENIBER ELECTIONS.

The request that has been made by Parlia- ment and passed on to Warrington by the Mayor, that, in the circumstances, there should be as little friction as possible this November, has been met by the three Labour organisations in Warrington in a curious way. They have decided to contest three wards, and have selected the candidates to fight those wards, but they express the hope that a conference may be called, at which a mutual arrangement may be agreed upon. As the Chairman of the Trades Council meeting said, "If the Liberals and Tories would give them a chance of represen- tation they would. not fight, but if not they would have to fight." We should like to see our Labour friends enter into the almost universal spirit of peace that reigns in internal matters at the present time, and we think the town would appreciate such an attitude in a practical manner when the clouds have passed away; but if the parties concerned think their demand for representation does not admit of delay, they, of course, are the best judges. The wards they propose to attack are Bewsey (retiring member, Councillor Dickenson); Orford, Councillor J. Barnwell ; St. John's, Councillor Lashford. The first two are Liberals and the third a Conser- vative.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
NO CONTROVERSY.

We rejoice to see that such responsible men on the other side as the Rector, Alderman Bennett and Mr. H. J. Westbrook have paid generous tribute to the men who have managed the diplomatic and military affairs for the nation in this present crisis. The spirit in which this has been done will be valued and remem- bered. At the same time there seems a disposition to say, as the Rector said on Sunday night, and as Alderman Bennett says in an article we re- produce to-day, that National Service is needed in England, and that the war proves it. We think our friends should leave that highly con- troversial question alone for the present. We are prepared to argue on the other side, in the light of what has been achieved under a purely voluntary system, that it would be disastrous to upset present conditions, but we would rather not. Friends of National Service, sincere though they be, would be well advised not to make the present time of co-operation and peace a time for lamentation and innuendo. We can fight that question out later.

iIOY SCOUTS.

"An Old Journalist" writes:—

These are more popular than ever—and don't they know it, just ! I am reminded of a story of a review of a boys' brigade which Lord Roberts was to have held at Glasgow. At the eleventh hour, " Bobs " was obliged to cancel the engagement, and, although a substitute of some eminence was forthcoming the promoters considered it but right to notify ticket-pur- chasers that the famous soldier would not be present. In one instance they sadly overrated the effect of the announcement. A small youth who was to take part in the parade had come to get two tickets for his parents. The manager told him as gently as he could of the disappoint- ment. The lad did not seem at all affected. "Oh," he said "it's no Laird Roberts that faither and mither are coomin' to see—it's me !" Seriously, however, the lads are, one and all, made of the right sort of stuff—otherwise, of course, they would not have to be scouts. According to the highest testimony, scouting has killed loafing and made lessons a pleasure. Boy Scouts who under the old order would have wasted their leisure or spent their idle moments in wilful damage to property have devoted themselves ardently to swimming, surveying, signalling, bridge-building, nature study, sick nursing,, and other useful pursuits; and since the outbreak of war have done splendid ser- vice in the way, not only of carrying messages, but of guarding bridges, reservoirs, &c. Phy- sically, morally and in all respects, the move- ment has benefited the lads individually and been of use to the country as a whole.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
ENGLAND, ARISE!

, England, arise! The dying cries of brave men call you forth To face the grave, the weak to save; to prove your Christian worth. The fields of God lie soaked in blood, beneath the summer skies: The hounds of Hell, with purpose fell, must ne'er prevail. Arise!

Without a sound, the world around beholds, with bated breath, With anxious fears and bitter tears, a struggle to the death. The fight is long, the foe is strong; the days are dark with doubt; The hosts of sin are closing in upon you. Up! About!

Defend you must that sacred trust, for which your fathers died, The trust of might, to guard the right, whatever may betide. Dream not to fail! Right shall prevail, though rocks in twain be riven, Though mountains shake and tempests break. Fight, in the cause of Heaven!

England, arise! Avenge those cries of anguish. You have power The sword to wield, the weak to shield in danger's darkened hour. Your arm is strong to vanquish wrong; your help is from the skies: " Faint not, nor fear, His arm is near." In His great cause, arise! J.S.

LYONS v. LIPTON.

Mr. Justice Sankey on Tuesday granted an interim injunction, on the application of Messrs. J. Lyons and Company, Ltd., restraining Messrs. Lipton, Ltd., their servants or agents, from speaking or writing and publishing any words to the effect or of the substance that J. Lyons and Company, Ltd., or the directorate thereof, is composed of Germans, and that by purchas- ing their commodities the public is assisting the enemies of Great Britain.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 12,1914.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

ALEXANDER MARSH NEXT WEEK.

Next week we shall have the celebrated Alex- ander Marsh and Co., including Miss Carrie Baillie, at the Royal Court, paying an annual return visit. The programme includes "Oliver Twist," "If I were King," "The Piper of Hamelin" followed by "Leah," "David Gar- rick" followed by "The Corsican Brothers," "Napoleon and His Washerwoman," "Sorrows of Satan," and "Nell Gwynne." In no place does the repute of this company stand higher than in Warrington, and we ex- pect that Mr. Marsh and his talented colleagues will find here, as they have found elsewhere, that war conditions will have no appreciably adverse effect upon the size of the houses they will attract.

It is a fine programme they submit, and they certainly command success. Special terms are offered for school parties.

" Keep the Workers Working!"

The Editor of the " Tatler " says :-" No pro- fession has responded more magnificently in the past to the sacred call of charity than the Stage. No profession is suffering more cruelly from the effects of this terrible war. So let us, if we can, support their present endeavours to give us re- laxation, and to keep as many of their members employed as is possible."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
DRAMA OF BUSH LIFE AT THE ROYAL COURT.

A drama from the prolific nen of Mrs. F. G. Kimberley can always be relied upon to rivet the attention of an audience, and this week's attraction at the Royal Court, which deals most realistically with life in the bush and is entitled "Australian Nell," does not lack in emotional situations and thrilling incidents. Australian Nell goes through much hardship and sorrow before she is finally restored to her loved ones, her honour established. Her love story with the "gentleman" gold-digger is the leading theme which connects up the various incidents in the play. Miss Leah Corenters, who has no mean repu- tation in Warrington, leaves nothing to be desired in her sympathetic portrayal of the heroine. As the disappointed Laura Carrington, Miss Gladys Morris scores a success, and the same result attends the efforts of Miss Evelyn Seymour, in the part of Phcebe. Kiddie, the child, is simply and suitably presented by Miss Ethel Masterman. Mr. Sidney Monckton makes a manly Arthur Carrington, and as the villain- ous Jed Angeles, Mr. Alfred D. Adams is most realistic. Mr. Morton Shields infuses consider- able vigour into the part of Jack Richardson, whilst Sambo (Mr. Fred Wright) and Arrabella (Miss Louie Heathfield) do not fail to amuse successfully. 4

===== 0002601_19140912_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
KARNO FUN AT HIPPODROME.

Patrons of the Hippodrome have on many occasions enjoyed the rare fun provided by those laughter-provoking sketches for which Fred Karno's companies enjoy such a wide reputa- tion. This week two of the very latest, "The Hydro" and "The New Slavey," are being pre- sented at this popular house in most excellent fashion by a company of inimitable mirth pro- viders. The comedy in .both is very rich, the antics of the " patients" taking the waters in the first-named sketch being excruciatingly funny. Dan Raynor is responsible for many outbursts of laughter, and the whole company combine together in a manner that cannot fail to be successful. "The New Slavey " is also a very diverting piece, and is much enjoye4 A high-class musical entertainment is provided by the Child Trio, who manipulate a variety of in- struments in a really clever manner. Dave Bry- ant is a smart Hebrew mimic with a distinctive style. Tom Gott again pleases with his York- shire comedy and his highly humorous denun- ciation of "mere man" from the point of view of the mere female. La Freya poses in most ar- tistic studies, including many of a patriotic character. War pictures on the cinema are also well received. Next Week. _ _

- Next week's programme will include: Mr. Ernest Dottridge's company in "The Dear Departed," by the late Stanley Houghton, the famous author of "Hindle Wakes"; Francis and Alfred, the juggler and his assistant; the Bros. Redmond, acrobatic comedians; Minnie Paget, the concertina queen; Mollie Baldwin, operatic soprano; Barlow and Brookes, speciality comedy duettists; Harry Edson, and his musical dog comedian, " Doc "; and the Gaumont Graphic, showing the latest war news.

Leather Trade Uncertainty. The position of the leather producer, says the Warrington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review," is still very complex and full of doubt, and he would be a very courageous man who ventured a forecast of the course of trade over the next three months. Most con- flicting opinions are met with both amongst sellers and buyers, and, whilst some are optim- istic, there are others who give just as logical reasons for believing that trade will be very scanty and difficult. The fact is that the trade is pitted against an altogether abnormal set of circumstances; trade landmarks have been swept away; the rules which used to be a guide to operations are useless, and the accumulated experience of the past is of little avail. The only safe motto appears to be the old one, " festina lente," until such time as matters have developed sufficiently to afford some clue as to what is likely to emerge from the present upheaval, and I am therefore inclined to think leather will not be produced abundantly, that prices will be maintained on a high level, and that all changes will be towards still higher figures. The turnover to-day is fairly good, owing to the late strike contracts failing very much into arrears, so that sole leather is leav- ing the tanneries freely. Apart from this, busi- ness is not important, as buyers are generally nervous about paying the new prices, averring that the demand of the home trade is very quiet. On the other hand, the demand for everything in medium to heavy weights suitable for Gov- ernment work is greater than the supply, and for this kind of leather there is not the smallest difficulty in makihg full prices. Of real Army bends there is a pronounced scarcity, and orders for such large quantities of Army boots are being placed that it seems the authorities may have to be less exigent in their stipulation as to the thickness of the leather used. Shoulders and bellies have been in very free demand, and some fair sales have been made at the advanced figures._ _ _ _

-Rough leather is generally quiet, the only classes in which any activity is noticeable being such as are suitable for the Walsall curriers.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KAlSER—continued.

have been eliminated, even if the empire could produce such.

A System That Must Die.

But it would be unfair to throw all the blame of the present conflict upon the Kaiser. He is in part the product of his environment, the re- sultant of the forces working in Germany since the Franco-Prussian War. The national ten- dency has been towards materialism, of which militarism and double dealing diplomacy are natural products. German diplomatic and mili- tary methods are the natural outcome of the warped mental and moral bias which- has in late years become grafted upon the national character. The prifoitive savagery of the army, which to some may appear a sudden lapse, is the result of the gradual disintegration of moral restraints and ideals in the national life. The cynical faithlessness, the lawless conduct of the army in the field, the destruction of world treasures, the worship of force, are all symp- toms of this moral decay. The results of this decadence has aroused the burning wrath, as well as the sorrow, of the world. This is what has turned Europe into a vast slaughter field and imperilled the highest acquisitions of civilisation. The foundations of our national and international organisms have been terribly shaken, and the peace of Europe must never again be at the, mercy of such a restless, ill- balanced, and unscrupulous state or ruler. A nation that has so relapsed into Paganism should pass into the depths of oblivion. Ger- many has become an enemy to the common- wealth of nations, she has outlawed herself, and as such must be treated. Sho must be rendered politically and militarily innocuous, her power to do evil must be taken away, else the future is red with ruin for European civilisation. There is no middle way.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KAISER.

A Decayed Ambitious Mind That Caused the War.

UNFIT FOR POWER.

BY C. E. PARKER.

Delineation of character is always difficult, and the difficulty increases with the complexity of the individual nature. The character of the Kaiser is far more perplexing than the com- paratively simple dual nature drawn by R. L. Stevenson in his Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde. To portray faithfully and adequately the multi- plex' and contradictory personality of William of Germany is probably beyond my powers, and present conditions increase the difficulty. Hence it is well that I should rely upon what compe- tent observers said of him before he startled the world by plunging Europe into war. An article on William 11. appeared in the English " Contemporary" in 1892. This created con- siderable stir at the time, not alone for its ability, but also for its truth. It was attributed with good reason to one Lothar Bucher, a friend of Bismarck. Of this article Bismarck said: "Whoever wrote it knew what he was writing about, for what he states is true." In thiA article we find the following : "Perhaps the most ominous joint product of the Emperor's vanity and superficiality combined is the megalomania (Grossen- wahn), which he seems to be developing at an alarming rate. This is not surprising, for megalomania is the diseased estimate of the relative proportion of things. . It is this megalomania which causes the deepest anxiety in Germany, because it is feared that it may lead to some irreparable piece of want of tact and thence to war. For it is argued that), vanity being at the bottom of it all, and the Emperor finding he is unable to gain the premature immortality he thirsts for by peaceful prodigies, his rest- less, nervous irritability may further in- crease and degenerate into recklessness, and then his megalomania may blind him to the dangers he, and, above all, poor, blood- soaken Germany must encounter on the war path." This was written twenty years ago, and what was feared has come to pass. . . .

A close associate of Biemarck tells us: "He (Bismarck) looked with grave mis- gifing on the Emperor's demonstrative ad- vances to Austria. Knowing the true nature of Austrian diplomacy, he was afraid that his impulsiveness would lead him to com- promise Germany, and allow her to be made

a cat's paw to -holster up Austrian inter- ests. Bismarck was for safeguarding the Austro-Hungarian Empire against unpro- voked attack, but not for allowing her to call the tune and drag Germany into the cycle of her dynastic interests at the risk of

Germany's very existence." What Bismarck feared thirty years ago has taken place. But I doubt if the order in the thought should not now be reversed to fit the probable facts. Should it not be put: "The Kaiser has called the tune and dragged Austria into the cycle of Germany's dynastic interests at the risk of Austria's very existence"? The hands are the hands of Austria, but the voice is that of Germany. Anyhow, both Powers are responsible, as the following extract from the German White Paper shows:—

_ "We [Germany] were able with all our hearts to inform our ally that we shared her opinion of the situation, and we assured her of our approval for any action which she might take to put an end to the movement in Servia directed against the integrity of the Monarchy. We were well aware that any military action by Austria against Servia might bring Russia on the scene, and involve us in war by reason of the obliga- tions of our alliance. Realising, as we slid, that• the vital interests of Austria-Hungary were at stake, we could neither counsel our ally to a pliability inconsistent with her dignity, nor refuse her our aid in this diffi- cult moment."

Divine Right Claim. But I am straying from my purpose. The extract from the "Contemporary," while true as far as it goes, dwells altogether upon the weak and dangerous traits of the Kaisei's char- acter. But are not these responsible for the present catastrophe? The danger of a mon- archy practically absolute is that a monarch who has a "diseased estimate of the relative proportion of things" has the power to involve a Continent in war. A child of four with a I loaded revolver is a small danger compared with this. But surely something better can be said of the Kaiser than the foregoing, and he is not without his admirers and apologists. He is described by one as the greatest religious force in the world, and this is spoken of as the key to his character. He is looked upon, and is, in many respects, a Puritan, although he rests his claim upon the divine right of kings. He accepts his kingship as God's appointment, and acknowledges that to Him he is responsible for its use. He is a model husband and father, a king the splendour of whose court is untar- nished by any moral laxity. He has declared that "the foundation of the Empire is laid in the fear of God." His belief in the immanence of God in the affairs of the world, and especially of Germany, is very real. He tells us : "God reveals Himself, now in this, now in that, great sage, whether it be priest or king, whether it be among heathens, Jews, or Christians. Hummurabi was one of ' these, and so were Moses, Abraham, Homer, Charlemagne, Luther, Shakespeare, Goethe, Kant, the Emperor William the Great. God has certainly revealed Himself to divers persons in divers ways corresponding to the position of a nation and the standard of Civilisation it has attained, and he still does

so.in our day." Springing out of this belief, he claims in effect that God not only revealed Himself to Moses and to his grandfather, " The Emperor William the Great," but also to the latter's grandson, William. But hi q belief in the Deity assumes strange forms. God, to his mind, seems not so much the beneficent ruler of the whole world, as the one upon whom the Kaiser has conferred the distinction of being the God of Germany, at any rate, her special ally.

He said in 1961: " We will be everywhere victorious, even if we are surrounded by enemies on all sides, and even if we have to fight superior numbers, for our most powerful ally is God, who, since the time of the Great Elector and Great King, has always been on our side."

The American " Outlook " contrasts this utter- ance with the reply made by Abraham Lincoln, who, when asked during the darkest hours of the Civil War, whether he was sure that God was on "our side," said I do not know; I have not thought about that. But lam very anxious to know whether we are on God's side." The Kaiser's recent message, referring to the Crown Prince, "1 rejoice with you in Wilhelm's first victory. Flow magnificently God supported him !" suggested to Mr. Barry Pain the follow- ing lines addressed to the Kaiser "Led by Wilhelm, as you tell, God has done extremely well; You with patronising nod Show that you approve of God. Kaiser, face a question new, This—' Does God approve of you?'" This is rather rough on the Kaiser, and, per- haps, not quite fair, but William 11. has brought it on himself. In turn the Kaiser has filled many parts, that of poet, author, dramatist, art critic, artist, architect, soldier, sailor. To some he is a great lover of peace, and at one time he was consid- ered to be the greatest guarantee we had for European peace. On occasion he can compli- ment the Sultan and avow friendship for him, while that potentate's hands are red with the blood of Armenians. "There is only one law, and that is my will," is one of his characteristic sayings.

Bismarck subordinated everything to policy, and his policy was the consolidation of Ger- many. The Kaiser subordinates everything to William IL The position is all the more un- fortunate and dangerous because Germany has produced no outstanding statesman since the enforced retirement of Bismarck, and the push- ful influence of the Kaiser had become the dominating factor, and there are none strong enough to control it. All strong personalities (Continued in preceding column.)

===== 0002601_19140912_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH ASCENDANCY.

SIR JOHN FRENCH'S PRAISE FOR

Ms ARMY.

The following was issued by the Press Bureau on Sunday

Sept. 6, 1914, 3 p.m. It is now possible to make another general survey, in continuation of that issued on August 30th, of the operations of the British Army dur- ing the last week. No new main trial of strength has taken place. There have indeed been battles in various parts of the immense front which, in other wars, would have been considered operations of the first magnitude; but, in this war, they are merely the incidents of the strategic withdrawal and contraction of the allied forces necessitated by the initial shock on the frontiers and in Bel- gium, and by the enormous strength which the Germans have thrown into the western theatre, while suffering heavily through weakness in the eastern.

The British Expeditionary Army has con- formed to the general movement of the French forces and acted in harmony with the strategic conceptions of the French General Staff. Since the battle at Cambrai on August 26, where the British troops successfully guarded the left flank of the whole line of French Armies from a deadly turning attack supported by enormous force, the 7th French Army has come into opera- tion on our left, and this, in conjunction with the sth Army on our right, has greatly taken the strain and pressure off our men. The sth French Army in particular on August 29 advanced from the line of the Oise River to meet and counter the German forward move- ment, and a considerable battle developed to the south of Guise. In this the sth French Army gained a marked and solid success, driv- ing back with heavy loss and in disorder three German Army Corps, the 10th, the Guard, and a reserve corps. It is believed that the Com- mander of the 10th German Corps was among those killed.

In spite of this success, however, and an the benefits which flowed from it, the general re- tirement to the south continued and the German armies, seeking persistently after the British troops, remained in practically continuous con- tact with our rearguards.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ACTION OF COMPIEGNE.

On August 30 and 31 the British covering and delaying troops were frequently engaged, and on September 1 a very vigorous effort was made by the Germans, which brought about a sharp action in the neighbourhood of Compiegne. This action was fought principally by the let British Cavalry Brigade and the 4th Guards Brigade and was entirely satisfactory to the British.

The German attack, which was most strongly pressed, was not brought to a standstill until much slaughter had been inflicted upon them and until 10 German guns had been captured. The brunt of this creditable affair fell upern our Guards Brigade, who lost in killed and wounded about 300 men.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR LOSSES 15,000.

_ After this engagement our troops were no longer molested. Wednesday, September 2, was the first quiet day they had had since the battle of Mons on August 23. During the whole of this period marching and fighting had been con- tinuous, and in the whole period the British casualties had amounted, according to the latest estimates, to about 15,000 officers and men. The fighting, having been in open order upon a wide front with repeated retirements, bas led to a large number of officers and men and even small parties missing their way and getting separated, and it is known that a very con- siderable number of those now included in the total will rejoin the colours safely. These losses, though heavy in so small a force, have in no wise affected the spirit of the troops. They do not amount to a third of the losses in- flicted by the British force upon the enemy, and the sacrifice required of the Army has not been out of proportion to its military...achievements.

. . In all drafts amounting to 19,000 men have reached our Army or are approaching them on the line of communication, and advantage is being taken of the five quiet days that have passed since the action of September 1 to fill up the gaps and refit and consolidate the units.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW GERMAN MOVEMENT.

The British Army is now south of the Marne, and is in line with the French forces on the right and left. The latest information about the enemy is that they are neglecting Paris and are marching in a south-easterly direction to- wards the Marne and towards the left and centre of the French line.

The Ist German Army is reported to be be- tween La Ferte-sous-Jouarre and Essises Vof- fort. The 2nd German Army, after taking Reims, has advanced to Chateau-Thierry and to the east of that place. The 4th German Army is reported to be marching south on the west of the Argonne, between Suippes and Ville sur Tourbe. All these points were reached by the Germans on September 3. The 7th German Army has been repulsed by a French Corps near D'Einville. It would, there- fore, appear that the enveloping movement upon the Anglo-French left flank has been abandoned by the Germans, either because it is no longer practicable to continue such a great extension or because the alternative of a direct attack upon the allied line is preferred. Whether this change of plan by the Germans is voluntary or whether it has been enforced upon them by the strategic situation and the great strength of the Allied Armies in their front will be revealed by the course of events.

GERMANS KNOW WE ARE SUPERIOR. There is no doubt whatever that our men have established a personal ascendancy over the Ger- mans, and that they are conscious of the fact that with anything like even numbers the result would not be doubtful. The shooting of the German infantry is poor, while the British rifle fire has devastated every column of attack that has presented itself. Their superior training and intelligence has enabled the British to use open formations with effect, and thus to cope with the vast numbers employed by the enemy. The cavalry, who have had even more opportu- nities for displaying personal prowess and ad- dress, have definitely established their superior- ity. Sir John French's reports dwell on this marked superiority of the British troops of every arm of the service over the Germans. "The cavalry," he says, "do as they like with the enemy until they are confronted by thrice their numbers. The German patrols simply fly before our horsemen. The German troops will not face our infantry fire, and as regards our artillery they have never been opposed by less than three or four times their umbers,"

INCIDENTS OF BRITISH VALOUR. The following incidents have been men- tioned :

thDe uwrhinogletohie tahcetioonfficaetrsLeanCdatmeaenu ooin oAnuegoufsttg Britgh batteries had been killed or wounded with the exception of one subaltern and two gunners. These continued to serve one gun, kept up a sound rate of fire, and came unhurt from the battlefield. On another occasion a portion of a supply column was cut off by a detachment of German cavalry and the officer in charge was summoned to surrender. He refused, and starting his motors off at full speed dashed safely through, losing only two lorries. It is noted that during the rearguard action of the Guards Brigade on September 1 the Ger- mans wounded. e seen giving assistance to our The weather has been very hot with an almost tropical sun, which has made the long marches trying to the soldiers. In spite of this they look well and hearty, and the horses, in conse- quence of the amount of hay and oats in the fields, are in excellent condition. In short, it may be said that the war so far as it has advanced has given most promising op- portunities of adding te the reputation of the British arms and of achieving notable and sub- stantial successes; but we must have more men so as to operate on a scale propoitionate to the strength and power of the Empire.

A PURE FOOD. For the fifth successive year " ATORA " Re- fined Beef Suet has been awarded the Certificate of the Incorporated Institute of Hygiene for the highest Standard of Quality. Although "ATORA " contains no preservative, it keeps sweet for months. No chopping required; goes nearly twice as far as raw Suet. Your grocer sells it—Shredded (coated with rice flour) for puddings and pastry and in blocks for frying and cooking. Ask for " ATORA," refuse substituted brands.

German Prisoners Leave the Workhouse.

It was rrted at a meeting of the Warring- ton Board of Guardians on Wednesday that the twelve German prisoners had been discharged from the house.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
More Recruits.

Almost every day recruits are being dispatched to all parts of the country from Orford Barracks and the scenes at the station as each batch has departed have been encouraging.

Officials and the War. It was resolved at the meeting of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday, that the difference between Army pay and the salaries of the Union officers who have gone to the war should be made up, exclusive of any emoluments.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Conservative Training Corps.

The Conservative Association maintains its activity, and a large number of men are being trained and drilled all through the week. Up to yesterday about 400 men had joined the prepara- tory training corps.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
Church Members Recruit.

At the close of the intercession service held on Sunday evening at the Wycliffe Congregational Church, the Rev. T. T. James read out a list of about fifteen names of Wycliffe lads who, hav- ing joined the ranks, had left the town, to serve their country.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Debates in the Market.

Market day on Wednesday afforded the ladies of the town an excellent opportunity—of which they fully availed themselves for exchanging general views upon the war. Many of the pun- ishments which, they declared, should be meted out to the Kaiser did credit to their powers of invention.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Extra Cost of Police.

At the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday a letter was read from the County Treasurer stating that owing to the heavy cost of police, due to the war, it would be necessary to increase the amount required for the second half of the linancial year by a rate of one penny in the X.

Hard Lines!

In Bridge-street the other evening, a be-spec- taeled young Warringtonian was heard com- plaining bitterly at not being allowed to enlist, "just because" he wore spectacles. "'Why." quoth he, indignantly, "in the German army, three out of every five men wear them, and they fight all right."—Do they, though? Special Constables' Tokens.

Several *arringtonians who are prevented from joining the Army by various circumstances have done the next best thing by offering their services to the town as special constables. So that others may know that they are doing what they can for their country in this critical period it has been suggested that these men should agree each to wear a blue and white striped button in his lapel.

Patriotic Hockey Players. At a general meeting of the Latchford Hockey Club, held on Wednesday evening, with Mr. Fox in the chair, it was unanimously decided to abandon all fixtures for the forthcoming season. It was noted with satisfaction that Messrs. R. A. Dale, H. Fletcher, W. S. Furness, A. Knight, A. G. Marsden, C. Newbold and F. C. Newbold were already on active service, whilst several other members had volunteered.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
To Save Church Festival Decorations.

The current number of the St. Ann's Parish Magazine states that this year the usual harvest thanksgiving decorations will be dispensed with. Asked by an "Examiner" representative as to the reason for such a decision, the Rev. James Davies said that in niany churches it had been arranged to use no corn in the decoration schemes, and he thought it advisable to go still further, and use nothing that could be saved.

A Changed Scene. One can hardly recognise O'Leary-street—very appropriately named, by the way, considering that it runs past the local military headquar- ters—as that which every day last week was crowded with recruits and relations, patiently waiting for the Warrington " Tommies " to march away to the station. Now that they have gone—in the best of spirits, of course—wives and mothers calmly resume their everyday tasks, and the neighbourhood is quiet once more.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
Martiai Music in Demand.

Patriotic songs and military music are in I great demand nowadays, and the stocks of War- rington music-dealers have been largely drawn on. One local music-seller informed us yester- day that he sold his last copy of "The Marseil- laise" over a week ago. "It seems to be the most popular song at present," said he. "Rag- time hasn't got a look in!" In most of our schools, also, the singing lessons are devoted to the National Anthems and patriotic airs of the Allies.

Two Wars in Progress. "There are two wars going on at the present time," said the secretary of a large Warring- ton firm of ironfounders yesterday, conversing with an "Examiner" representative. "In addi- tion to 'the blood and iron' struggle, we are waging a commercial war with Germany, and own n aretow going e rte o arew i nt signs f Already, tion for his not unduly sanguine opinion, in at i n even in our victory." Justifica- tion least one respect, rests in the fact that his firm have this week received a large and important order hitherto executed in Germany.

Teachers and Old Scholars Enlist. The five male teachers at the Beamont Coun- cil Schools, Warrington—Messrs. D. Morgan James, Reginald A. Dale, Harold Beach, Thomas S. Savage and W. Stanley Furness—who, as we stated last week, offered themselves for military service at home or abroad, left the town on Monday to join the Army Ordnance Corps. They were given a hearty send-off by the re- mainder of the staff, and their places have since been filled by lady teachers. The Headmaster (Mr. W. Mason) has also traced 26 old Beamont scholars who are serving in the Army and Navy.

For King and Country. Mr. Paul Przeger, formerly of Warrington, has been granted a commission as lieutenant and quartermaster in Lord Kitchener's Army. He is the second son of the late Herr Prceger, who was well known in Warrington, and joined the Army in June, 1889. During the 24 years Ile was in the 9th Queen's Royal Lancers, he served In emy rank Trom private to sergeant- major, retiring in 1913. He saw active service in the Matabele War of 1896, and also in the South African War, when he earned four medals, including the Queen's South African medal and seven clasps. His brother, Sergeant Arthur Prceger, also in the 9th Lancers, is now serving with the Expeditionary Force on the Con- tinent. It is this regiment that last week was reported as having made a charge which, for sheer heroism, is only equalled by the immortal dash of the deathless 600 at Balaclava.

The Mayor's Poster. A poster, in red, white and blue, decorated at the head with the Royal Standard and the Union Jack, and bearing the following wording, has been issued by the Mayor:— ' County Borough of Warrington. " Lord Kitchener's Army. "Lord Derby is coming to Warrington as soon as he is restored to health, to address the men of Warrington on the subject of the war, and to raise a Warrington battalion of the above army. The able-bodied men of Warrington should not miss this splendid opportunity of serving their country in a glorious cause. " The battalion will consist of about 1,000 men, divided in companies of about 100. If the bat- talion is raised, it will enable all men who work together in the shops, and all pals and chums, to enlist together in any of the companies, and by signing on together in bodies of ten they , can occupy the same tent and remain together, and, if need be, fight together throughout the campaign. "-Now is your opportunity of forming a War- rington battalion of 'Comrades in Arms.' "PETER PEACOCK, Mayor." " There is but one task for all : For each one life to give. Who stands if Freedom fall? Who dies if England live?" —Kipling, Sept.,. 1914. "God save the King."

Liberal Bowlers.

In a friendly match played between the BulL's Head and the Liberal Club teams on Thursday, the latter were beaten by 97-56 on doubles and 160-99 on the single games

New Office for the Mayor,..

At a meeting of the Insurance Conamittee held on Thursday evening the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) was elected chairman for the en- suing year by eight votes as against seven cast for last year's chairman, Dr. Joseph.

Padgate Choir Sermons. The Wesleyan choir anniversary was held on Sunday, when advantage was taken of the com- ing into the Warrington district of three new ministers to hear addresses from two of them— the Rev. W. Thompson Derry (superintendent minister of the circuit) and the Rev. Arthur J. Morton (resident minister).

===== 0002601_19140912_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of Vell-known Bowler.

A well-known local howler has passed away in the person of Mr. Harry Eyes, of 24, Willis- street. His demise occurred at the Warrington Infirmary on Thursday, after an operation. Deceased was a prominent county bowler and a member of the Bull's Head Bowling Club. He was a successful trundler and had won many

Separation Order. At the Borough Police Court yesterday morn- ing a separation order was granted against Mr. Tom Boothroyd, musical director, who did not appear, and he was ordered to pay the costs and ~,t1 per week to his wife, who is to have the custody of the children. The application was made on the ground of persistent cruelty. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared for the wife.

flounders Bowl for Wycliffe. Yesterday afternoon, at Wycliffe Council School, Mr. J. Fulton-Smith handed the chal- lenge silver rose-bowl for rounders to the cap- tain and brooches to the team members. The Headmaster (Mr. S. W. Jeffery) presented Mr. Osborne Sinclair, the school "sports enthusiast," with a traveller's portmanteau on behalf of the staff and scholars.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Alderman Honoured.

_ As a token of appreciation, respect and esteem Alderman and Mrs. Tinnion were presentfrd with a silver tea service by the members of the Liberal Club last night.—Councillor Wilkinson made the presentation and eulogistic speeches were given by Councillor Locker and Mr. W. Hayes.—Alderman and Mrs. Tinnion suitably responded and were accorded musical honours.

Wesleyan Quarterly Meeting. The quarterly meeting of the Warrington Wes- leyan Circuit was held at Padgate on Thursday, Mr. J. G. Withinshaw presiding. A cordial welcome was extended to the new ministers. The numerical and financial statements were both considered satisfactory, and a suggestion with reference to the location of the superintendent minister was , deferred until the next meeting. Thanks were accorded the officers and all were re-appointed. The next meeting will be at the Bold-street Church on December 10th.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
Market Prices.

The following were the prices ruling in War- rington Market on September 9th :—Wheat, 21s. to 21s. Bd. per 280th.; oats, 3s. Id. to 3s. 21d. per 451 b.; straw,47s. to 18s. per ton; hay, .22 15s. to £3 ss. per ton. There has been a general de- cline in the price of oats and wheat, the latter going back in price 2d. to 3d., with the likeli- hood of a further decline. Oats have also slackened off id. per bushel, good quantities being offered. Flour remains firm at 31s. per 2101 b.

A Wycliffe Wedding. At the Wycliffe Congregational Church on Thursday afternoon Miss Eva Elizabeth Wood, niece of Mr. Josiah Longland, solicitor, of Walton-road, Stockton Heath, was married to Mr. Harold Hawthorn, youngest son of the late Mr. Thomas Hawthorn, of Lovely-lane. The ceremony was conducted by the Rev. T. T. James. Miss Lilian Atkinson was the brides- maid, and Mr. P. 0. Bailey the best man. The bride was given away by Mr. Josiah Longland. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ and rendered suitable musical selections.

New Minister Welcomed. The Rev. W. Thompson Derry, the new Wes- leyan superintendent minister in Warrington, preached his first sermon at Bewsey:road Church on Sunday. On the following evening a public meeting was held in the church to formally welcome him and his wife. In re- turning his thanks for the greeting accorded him he assured the congregation that it would be his duty to lead the people into the immedi- ate presence of God. He was willing to work hand in hand with all the Free Churches, as well as with the Church of England, and with the other churches, if they, in turn, would work with him for the glory of God.

Funeral of the Late Mrs. Miss.

The funeral tooi—Pln7c;.;;-Wai.UrdaY last of Mrs. Twiss, widow of the late Mr. Edward Twiss, at the Hill Cliffe Burial Ground. Mrs. Twiss had been in delicate health for some little time, but the end came unexpectedly on September Ist. She was in her 76th year. Mr. William Twiss conducted the service at Hill Cliff() Chapel and at the graveside. LOCAL WAR ITEMS.

CONCERT FOR HOSPITAL FUNDS.

-.--... . A well-attended concert, arranged by the ' Latchford Orchestral Entertaining Society was held in Latchford on Thursday night for the purpose of providing additional funds for the Red Cross Hospital at Raddon Court. During an interval in the programme Miss S. Broad- bent addressed the audience on the work of the society, pointing out the need for funds at the present time. At the close the orchestra, which was conducted by Mr. A. Grounds, played the Marseillaise and the National Anthem, the audience standing whilst two Boy Scouts brought in the French and British flags.

Belgian. Refugee ~Babies.

The work of inquiry and of relief of the wives and dependents of Territorials, soldiers and sailors has been continued during the week at the Parr Hall. Many inquiries have been dealt with and information and advice given to all applicants. There was a rumour current that Belgian babies were being given away at Parr Hall. athere is no truth in this, although ap- plications have been received from persons will- ing to adopt Belgian children, and inquiries are 1 being made as to the practicability of this

Lecture on the War.

The Mayor has arranged for a very interest- ing lecture to be given in the Parr Hall on Tues- day evening, the 22nd instant. Further particu- lars will be given later. We may, however, say that the lecturer is Mr. W. Herbert Garrison, F.R.G.S., official lecturer to the Colonial Insti- tute, and that Ile will deal with the present European War, its causes, and its relation to Great Britain and the Empire. The lecture, which will be illustrated by lantern slides, will be free, but there will be a few reserved seats, for which a small charge will be made.

Under Martial Law.

An_ Accompaniment of Fame:

"I have just been reading Boswell's reminis- cences of Dr. Johnson." said the bookish—dian. "What!" exclaimed the man who is not bookish. "Has some college given the coloured pugilist a degree already?" How Dare We?

Reports still continue to come in as to out- bursts of rage which took place in Germany when the news of our participation in the war reached that country. Seeing that we had merely been asked to allow our friends to be robbed and murdered, our interference is looked upon as peculiarly gratuitous.—" Punch."

Not Recognised. The "Sheffield Daily Telegraph " : "A sow has given birth to a freak of nature. The animal's face is almost human in appearance; it has neither eyes nor nostrils, but a nose like a fish." " Punch ": "This is like none of our friends."

The Thinker. Cornerman, to companion: " You say this war don't affect you, but 'ow, instead of a British copper sayin" Git aht of it,' would yer like.one o' them German johndarms to keep proddin' at yer wif 'is baynit?"—" Punch."

Absent-minded? The Scotsman could not find his ticket. On the conductor's second round it was, still miss- ing. "What's that in your math?" he asked. Sure enough, there was the missing ticket. The conductor punched it, and went his way. "Ah, we'el," said Sandy, in reply to his fellow passen- gers' banter, "I'm nae sae absent-minded as ye wad think. Yon was a vera auld ticket and I was jist sucken aff the date."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Free Church Recruits.

Before leaving the town to undergo the course of training which is necessary before they can be sent out to the front, the Free Churchmen among the local recruits for Lord Kitchener's Army attended a special early morning service at Golborne.street Baptist Church on Sunday last. There was a large muster, and the ser- vice was of a stirring character. It was con- ducted by the Rev. H. Ogden, who, in the course of an inspiring address, said that he. rejoiced that whilst other young men were content to appear in football dress, those before him were anxious to wear the uniform that symbolised the struggle for freedom.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
OVER 2,000 FROM THE WORKS.

Many men from the larger Warrington works continue to recruit, and below we give a rough estimate of the numbers who have already offered their services :—Crosfield's, 540; Pearson and Knowles, 410; Rylands', 312; Whitecross, 200; Fletcher, Russell's, 150; Monks, Hall, 100; St. Helens Cable, 55; Richmond's 75; other works, 400; total, 2,242.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON WARRIORS.

Second Batch of Territorials for the Front.

A CIVIC FAREWELL.

Warrington gave another three hundred of her sons to the service of King and country on Monday. Despite the fact that they lacked kharki uniforms and the other trappings of , war, these gallant volunteers in the hour of 1 their country's need looked a soldierly lot as, drawn up in lines in front of the Town Hall, they stood at attention to listen to a few words of commendation and farewell from the Mayor. It was anticipated that they would have left Warrington on the Monday morning, but owing to some unforeseen difficulty in arranging for the necessary special train for their conveyance to Scotland—where, by the way, they have joined the contingent who left the town a week or two back—it was close upon 11 o'clock at night before they got away. However, despite the lateness of the hour, their departure was witnessed by a 1 • large crowd of people, but it was noticeable that there was a distinct absence of that raucous- voiced "patriotism" which generally marks such occasions. In a word, the leave-taking was a dignified one—just a few cheers and a few hand- shakes, a shriek of the engine's whistle, a final shout of "good luck" from friends and on- lookers, and the plucky lads were being whirled away on the first phase of their high enterprise. A few women-folk who had come to see loved ones off—perhaps to see them for the last time-- wept silently, but behind their grief was a sense of pride that their man, be he husband, son, brother or sweetheart, had heard the call of his country and had responded to it.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
WAITING TO GO.

The word went round on the Sunday evening that the men would be off first thing the next morning, and, accordingly, after an early and hasty breakfast, they made ready for the jour- ney. Eatables to sustain the inner man on the long railway journey ahead were procured and pocketed, and haversacks were packed with such belongings as were considered indispensable, and then the men sat down to await, with what patience they could command, the order to go. The morning wore on and on, and as still no word came, the Officer Commanding (Major Appleton) made arrangements for the men to leave their somewhat cramped and unlovely quarters in the Drill Hall and bivouac on the springy lawn at the rear of the Town Hall, a change of environment that was much appre- ciated. . .

Stretched at their ease on the green sward, the men enjoyed themselves with smoke and song. All the popular ditties were sung with great gusto, and at intervals there came spon- taneous outbursts of cheering. A large and admiring crowd gathered round the Park gates and railings to look on, and every now and then proud relatives arrived with refreshments for one or other of the plucky lads. The Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. P. Peacock), Alderman Bennett, Lady Greenall, and the Chaplain (the Rev. W. Bracecarop) mixed freely with the men as they sat basking in the sun.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR'S KINDLY ACT.

During the afternoon the contingent was marched back to the Drill Hall, where the Major and the Chaplain addressed them and where over 1,000 cigarettes were distribut!ed—a small present from the Mayor, betokening a kindly thought that was much appreciated. Later the lads were marshalled in lines in front of the Town Hall, on the same spot that their Territorial comrades occupied before they bade adieu to the town early on the morning of August 13th. Looking on from the steps of the Town Hall were the Mayor and Mayoress, Coun- cillor Joseph (ex-Mayor), Aldermen Smethurst and John Evans, Councillors Dickenson, Chrimes, J. Wilkinson and W. H. J. Jenkins, Messrs. T. Locker, J. Lyon Whittle, Ker, Made- ley, Woodcock, P. Garnett, J. H. Smethurst and H. Stuart Page, and the Rev. W. Bracecamp. At the sharp word of command the men stood at attention. Major Appleton advanced towards the facade of the Town Hall, and, with a salute, communicated the intelligence that his men waited the Mayor's pleasure, where- upon his Worship stepped forward, and in strident tones which easily penetrated to the rearmost rank, spoke a few appropriate words of commendation and farewell.

"WARRINGTON IS PROUD OF YOU." The MAYOR said : Officers and men of the 4th Battalion of the South Lancashire Regt,—Allow me, in the name of Warrington, to say how proud we are of you men volunteering at such short notice. Within twenty-four hours after we had received the telegram asking for 300 men you had come forward and answered the call, and we had to refuse a good many others because the numbers were made up. We, in Warring- ton, know that Warrington men are made 9f the real grit, and that they will discharge their duties to the full, with credit to the town which sent them, and in a manner which will be an encouragement to others to follow, if need be. Allow me to praise you very highly, because we asked you to join for foreign active service, , and you have answered the call nobly and well, maintaining the glorious traditions of the past. We all wish you "God speed" and a safe return ' and when the roll-call is again repeated here we hope to see you all looking, if possible, better in health than you are to-day. You are fighting against one of the most despicable Monarchs who ever trod this earth, and we know that right must in the end prevail. When the cheering had subsided the CHAPLAIN offered a special prayer for all those engaged in the war, and at the close pronounced the Bene- diction. He the asked the assembly to sing the Nat n National Anthem "as they had never sung it before," and after his request had been heartily complied with, Alderman BE:marr called for cheers for the Territorials, which were loudly given.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
DELAYED DEPARTURE.

Although his Worship's farewell was said. at 4.30 in the afternoon, fate decreed that the de- Parture of the men should not take place until a few minutes before eleven at night. At six o'clock they were ordered back to the Drill Hall, wheie the Major informed them that they would be dismissed until nine o'clock. This conces- sion was greatly appreciated. Soon after ten o'clock the 300 men left their Warrington head- quarters and marched through crowded streets to the Bank Quay Station. Outside the station barricades had been erected and a strong force of police were on duty to keep back the crowd, which, despite the lateness of the hour, was a fairly numerous one. As the marching men swung through the station doors friends pressed forward for a final handclasp. Entraining was speedily carried out, and as the men waited for the train to move off they crowded to the carriage windows to exchange parting words with such of their relatives as had been allowed the privilege of admission to the platform. Waiting on the station to see the boys off were the Mayor and Aldermen Tinnion and Bennett, and the Chaplain. As the train steamed slowly out of the station cheers were raised and farewells waved, and there was every indication that the gallant lads were in the best of spirits. Travelling with them, in addition to Major Appleton, who was, of course, in command, were Captain Roberts and Second Lieutenants Armstrong. and Holden.

These 300 men were needed to bring tho War- rington Territorial Foreign Service Battalion up to the full strength of 1,000.

LABOUR AND RECRUITING. Support for the Campaign from the Trades Council.

DISCUSSING CONDITIONS.

' What 1;1;1 should local-ija:l;Wir--iiit—e-rests take in the extensive recruiting schemes now in operation? This query was raised at the monthly meeting of the Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday night, when the following letter, dated in London on September 3rd, was read from Mr. Arthur Henderson:— " Dear Sir,—The crisis is so grave and the need for more recruits, in addition to the force of 100,000 just raised, is so vital to the interests of the country, that Lord Kitchener has inquired of the leaders of the great poli- tical parties if they would jointly agree that their organisations should give their full co- operation in securing the enlistment of more men. The Prime Minister extended the invi- tation to the Labour party to join in promot- ing this common purpose. A special meeting of the National Committee was held to consider the matter, when the following resolution was carried : " That in view of the serious situation created by the European War, the Executive Committee of the Labour party agree with the policy of the Parliamentary party in joining in the campaign to strengthen the British Army, and agrees to place the central offices' organisation at the disposal of the campaign, and further recommends the affiliated bodies to give all possible local support.' "All party differences are obliterated in our unanimous and all-absorbing purpose to rally to the support of those who are fighting hero- ically for their King and country, and I trust that you will unite with the Unionist and Liberal agents to give whatever assistance is possible in the work of recruiting in your con- stituency, particularly in helping to organise and carry through public meetings."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
POINTS TO BE CONSIDERED.

The Chairman (Mr. E. Handles) said the mat- ter should be thoroughly discussed because if there was a public meeting they should instruct their officials as to whether or not they should take part, if invited. Mr. Hamblett said he endorsed the attitude of the Parliamentary Committee, as he was thoroughly satisfied with it. He moved that the matter be adopted. Mr. W. J. Holden seconded. After a brief pause the Chairman queried: "Any amendment?" Mr. Helsby : We ought to have a further dis- cussion. We are establishing a precedent that requires a lot of consideration. I, for one, en- tirely agree that any young man who thinks he is in a position to volunteer for service should do so, but there are a good many actions the authorities are adopting which call for a strong protest from the Labour party. Some firms are practically discharging single young men and so trying to force them to join the Army, with- I out, in some cases, inquiring into the conditions of those men. These are things the Council can very well protest against We want to formulate some policy and say: "If we are going to help you in recruiting we want to be assured that the dependents and the crippiles will be cared for." We want it guaranteeing that the crippled men, when they come back, will be provided for without their imposing on charity. The Chairman: For my own part, I think that is the attitude the Labour party is taking up.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
SINK ALL DIFFERENCES.

Mr. Grierley said he thought it was their business to work one with another to bring about a successful finish to the war. He thought the time had passed for talking about setting precedents. They should sink all politics for the time being—(Mr. Helsby : Hear, hear)—and do all they could to encourage the men to serve their country. He supposed that if the Ger- mans came to England the workers would be no better off. Their business was to support the Government in bringing about a successful end- ing to the war. Their officials should be able to speak at meetings with the authority of the Council backing them up. Mr. Hamblett did not believe that the Labour party had entered upon the matter lightly. Mr. Chamberlain associated himself with Mr. Helsby. It was a material factor as to how the men were going to be provided for when they came back. They wanted to see that they would be treated in the best possible maatner. Many cases could be cited where people were suffering as a result of men volunteering to yo out. The same promises that were made during the Boer war were being made again to-day, but they wanted something definite. They all knew that some single young men had as much depending upon them as some married men.

THE CIVIL SERVICE. Mr. Healey said that last Saturday a public , man placed before him a view on "the recruit- ing business" which he, had not heard before, and he was not going to say the man was cor- rect. He stated that before any further re- cruiting was entered upon the Government could call upon the Civil Service men, all of whom, by the terms of their engagements, are liable to serve King and country in any way whatever. All these men could be called upon in time of emergency just as much as the re- serve of the Army. He (Mr. Healey) was not going to say that that was correct, but it was placed before him, as a serious proposition, by a public man, who was engaged every day in public ,life and who said that that point was being entirely overlooked. A Delegate stated that there were firms in the town which were not now giving the assur- ance that jobs would be kept open. When these promises were made it caused such a large number of young men to go away that it was bound to interfere with business. A young man required some kind of pledge as to what awaited him when he came back. Mr. Chamberlain asked if it was a fact that the money given by firms to the dependents of soldiers was taken into account when the relief fund was distributed?

Mr. Charles Dukes, after answering in the affirmative, drew the attention of the delegates to the wording of the resolution, which, he said, was extremely nebulous. They ought to have a definite policy. LABOUR PARTY'S STRENGTH..

The Chairman said if they endorsed the resolution they were instructing the Council to assist in organising meetings, when they could show labour's part in the recruiting, and bring pressure to bear upon the Government on behalf of those men who came back maimed, and do something to see that they were cared for and not afterwards thrown on the scrap heap. _ _

Mr. Hamblett said he readily recognised the gravity of the situation. As a member of that Council he had closely followed the attitude of the Labour party, who, he believed, had not come to their decision lightly. His point was that their Labour friends in Belgium and France were asking for assistance. He certainly did not agree with war; he believed it was hel- lish and devilish. (Hear, hear.) *lf men came back maimed or weakened in any way it would be disgusting if they were not properly cared for. However, he believed that the Labour party was to-day stronger than ever and would not allow it. (Hear, hear.) The resolution was carried by an overwhelm- ing majority.

PLAYING AT SOLDIERS BECOMES REALITY.

Church Lads' Brigade Members at the Front.

VALUABLE HELP BEING RENDERED TO

TLIE NATION. There are a large number of the members of the local battalion of the Church Lads' Brigade amongst the Warrington Territorials, and also amongst the recruits who left the town on

Monday evening. The figures compare favour- ably with other towns, and bring the aggregate to no less than 5,000 officers, warrant officers, and older lads, who are serving with the colours. The governing body feel deeply gratified with this, and are now engaged in an endeavour to raise one or more battalioas for Lord Kitchener's Army, which, should be composed entirely of former members of the C.L.8., who are between the ages of 19 and 35. The War Office have sanc- tioned the scheme.

"Nothing," says a communique issued from the headquarters of the Brigade, "could more clearly emphasise the really magnificent work for the country which the Church Lads' Brigade has been continuously carrying on during the last twenty years. Its primary object is of a 'religious nature, it is true; none the less, the Imilitary method adopted so far from being a Imere playing at soldiers (as it has sometimes been disparagingly described), is proving itself to-day an obvious national asset, just at the very moment when such an asset is valuable beyond almost anything else." Headquarters are already in possession of no less than 1,500 applications from ex-members of the C.L.8., who are keenly desirous of serving their King and country in this truly patriotic fashion. It is more than probable, however, that there are many other ex-members living in all parts of the British Isles who are as yet wholly ignorant of this new and important step on the part of their much loved C.L.B. Any such are kindly requested to place themselves in communication with as little delay as pos- sible with the Rev. Jocelyn Perkins, honorary director of Special Services, Aldwych House, Catherine-street, Aldwych, London, W.C.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
STILL SHIRKERS IN THE TOWN. Will They Be Shamed Into Doing Something?

The Rev. W. Bracecamp had a word to say about the shirkers at a special service at St- , Paul's Church on Sunday morning, when a large number of the Territorials were present, under the command of Major Appleton. The rev, gentleman, who is chaplain to the local "Terriers," said that during the past few days the South Lancashires had acquitted them- selves nobly and bravely, and had fought against tremendous odds. He was sure those men who had joined the 4th Battalion of the South Lan- cashire Regiment would uphold the prestige of the regiment with honour to themselves and credit to the town to which they belonged. When the people of Warrington saw them march off they would admit that they were some of the town's best. Unfortunately there were still a number of shirkers in Warrington, but he believed the example of the recruits who had come forward would shame them into doing some service for their King and country. At the conclusion of the service the National Anthem was sung, and as the men left the edi- fice they were each presented with a hymn card.

LORD DERBY'S VISIT. The Mayor has been in communication with Lord Derby's Secretary, and it is hoped that his Lordship will be sufficiently recovered to attend a public meeting in Warrington next week. _ _ _

Between £7OO and £BOO is needed altogether. THE MOBILISATION FUND. To the Editor of "The Examiner." Sir,—Will you kindly allow me to use your columns publicly to express the thanks of the battalion under my command to those who so kindly and promptly subscribed to the fund of the battalion for the purchase of necessities for the men and for other expenses incurred on mobilisation which are not covered by grants from public funds? Warrington Company Army Service Corps, £2OO. Purchase, 2,011 pairs boots, 151 dozen shirts, 500 towels, 135 pairs wool socks, braces, razors, brushes (tooth, hair, shav- ing), knives (table and clasp), and numerous other items. Up to date, £1,798 has been ex- pended, and further expenditure will be neces- sary. The fund, however, will carry the battalion through if no more extraordinary calls are made on it, as on some of th'e items of expendi- ture it is hoped that a partial refund may be obtained.

The efficiency of the battalion is very greatly enhanced by the provision of the above, which are greatly appreciated by the men.—Yours, &c., BRERETON FAIRCLOUGH, Lieutenant-Colonel, Commanding 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment. September 3. 1914.

MAYOR'S NIECE MARRIED.

Big Crowd at Pretty Wedding at St. Paul's LIST OF WEDDING PRESENTS.

All classes of Warrington people felt a more than ordinary, nay, almost a personal, inter- est in a pretty wedding, which was solemnised at St. Paul's Church on Thursday afternoon, by reason of the fact that the bride, Miss Gladys Blanche Wilkinson, is the niece of the civic head of the borough, his Worship. Alderman Peter Peacock, the bridegroom being Mr. George Bebbington, the second sari of the late Mr. William Bebbington and of 'Ars. Bebbing- ton, who resides at 10, Marson-street. The great amount of interest displayed in the nuptials was evidenced by the large congregation, mainly composed of ladies--as is usual on such felicitous occasions—which gathered to witness the ceremony, practically every seat on the floor of the spacious church being occupied, whilst there were also many people in the ga'leries. I Still, large as was the number of those who made their way into the church, even more re- mained outside, content to get a glimpse of the bride and bridegroom on their arrival and de- parture. Shortly after two o'clock the bride drove up to the church, accompanied by her uncle, and, with her veil down and leaning on his Worship's arm, she made her way into the sacred edifice between two deep rows of inter- ested onlookers.

VICAR'S ADDRESS TO THE YOUNG COUPLE. The service was conducted by the Vicar (the Rev. William Bracecamp), who was assisted by the Rev. E. Downham (curate). It opened with the familiar hymn, "The voice that breathed o'er Eden."

After the ring had been placed on the bride's , finger and the happy couple were duly pro- nounced husband and wife, the Vicar, standing before the altar, addressed to them a few words of sage advice. He reminded them that there were marriages and marriages. There were those who went to church and were married, who did not seek for any ideal, but on the other hand, there were those marriages that had been thought out carefully, and, judging from his own knowledge of the two young people before him, he was confident that their marriage came in the latter category. He was certain that they had each made a wise choice, and h_e_hoped that throughout their lives they would be able to look back upon that day as one of the hap- piest, if not the happiest, in their lives. If they really wanted to be happy, as he was sure they did, he urged them to remember that the Lord must be with them "in all their goings out and comings in."

THE COSTUMES DESCRIBED. The bride, who was given away by the Mayor, looked very charming in her gown of ivory satin charmeuse, draped with Limerick lace, and embroidered with pearls. From both shoulders fell a handsome Court train, which was secured by ropes of pearls. It was of ivory brocaded satin, lined with pink ninon, and was trimmed with trails of orange blossom and caught up at one corner with lovers' knots of silver tissue and sprays of the same flowers. Her beautiful veil of Brusselslace, the gift of her aunt, Miss S. A. Peacock, was arranged in cap form, and she wore a wreath of orange blossom, myrtle and white heather, and carried a bouquet of orchids, the gift of Alderman Bolton. She was accompanied by two little train-bearers, Master Jackie Langley and Miss Dora Peacock, her cousins. Little Miss Peacock looked very pretty in a dainty white muslin and Irish lace frock, with a French cap to match. Master Jackie Langley wore a smart white sailor suit.

The bride was attended by three maids—Miss Elsie Wilkinspn and Miss Inez Wilkinson (her sisters), and Miss Kate Bebbington (sister of the bridegroom). All three were garbed in pretty dresses of rose-pink chiffon taffeta, draped with shadow lace and ninon, and each tore a black velvet picture hat, trimmed with single large begonias. They carried bouquets of white chrysanthemums and wore tourmaline and pearl rings, the gifts of the bridegroom. The Mayoress was charmingly gowned in a beautiful shade of pink silk, shot with silver. The skirt was made with the fashionable long over tunic, the corsage being composed of fine lace veiled over with pink chiffon. She also wore a medici collar of lace and chiffon_ Mrs. Bebbington, the mother of the bride- groom, wore a charming gown of black satin charmeuse, draped with chiffon and white shadow lace, with a bonnet to match. She car- ried a bouquet of pink carnations. Mr. Wm. Bebbington acted as groomsman to his brother.

Whilst the congregation were assembling the organist, Mr. Ernest Wilkes, played a number of short selections, and as the bridal party left the church after the ceremony he gave a spirited rendering of Mendelssohn's inAffcrrtal Wedding March.

After a reception, which was held at Atkin. son's Cafe, Mr. and Mrs. Bebbington left for their honeymoon, which is being spent in North Wales. For travelling the bride wore a charm- ing navy gabardine costume, with a waistcoat of Libellule cerise taffetas, and a hat to har- monise.

In celebration of the occasion the employes of Peacock's Bazaar Co. were given a holiday.
