Novel Reading. I do not by any means share the apparently grave concern of Warrington's excellent Alder- " man over the fact that a very considerable per- centage of the books required at the Library by the reading public are works of fiction. I suggest that it is wholly good that so many of our people should be found cultivating the habit of reading. Even assuming for the moment that a rather large proportion confine themselves almost exclusively to fiction, many of these will assuredly in due course pass to the study of more solid literature. It is, however, too much to suppose that comparatively few of them read only romance. I write from experience. As a youngster I absorbed and saturated myself with the great novels of the Victorian era, but when I came to form my little library at home, only a representative few of these had places on the shelves. My treasures are mainly classics; as fresh novels of which one must know some- thing are issued. I obtain a copy at the public library. I imagine that to be a common prac- tice. If that be granted—and it can hardly be seriously called in question—dogmatism with the librarian's statistics as sole text is unwar- ranted, and the resultant pessimism is a mis- take.

"Literary Fanatics." As the self-constituted mouthpiece of the people at large Councillor Pemberton resents dictation by "literary fanatics." Let him hear what Matthew Arnold saith : "Culture is indis- pensably necessary, and culture is reading; but reading with a purpose to guide it, and with system. He does a good work who does anything to help this; indeed, it is the one essential ser- vice now to be rendered to education." I know that Butler is cited as saying that really, in general, no part of our time is more idly spent than the time spent in reading; and I seem to remember Lord Rosebery holding forth in a similar strain. Without qualification, such dicta may by multitudes be misunderstood. The truth of the matter, as it seems to me, is that one of the great purposes of reading is to furnish materials for thinking. I have a friend who, in addition to good books, daily takes a dip into two newspapers of different politics. In the one he finds distinct stateinents which in the other are flatly contradicted, thus demand- ing clear and definite thought on his part. It is not for one in such circumstances to throw both papers down with the despairing comment that it is impossible to know what to believe. Readers must think for themselves, unless they are content to take their opinions at second- hand; they must sift the contradictions and de- termine for themselves which is right and which is wrong.

The Need of Guides. Councillor Pemberton's amusing phrase never- theless, people must have literary leaders, for of course only a small minority have leisure to become experts. So much at least was implied in last week's local item headed " Bible or music hall." By-the-bye, the Rev. Malcolm Peart once pointed out in "The Treasury" that the pun is one of the commonest forms of humour in the East. "The Old Testament writers, especially the prophets, are true Orien- tals in this respect, for they use this ornament of speech most effectively." In illustrating this, he threw a flood of light on one passage in refer- ence to Amos, the desert prophet's, vision of summer fruit, of which I had theretofore been unable to make anything at all. Here it is: "And he said, Amos, what seest thou? And I said, A basket of summer fruit. Then said the Lord unto me, The end is come upon my people Israel." For years and years the point was en- tirely lost on me; now, thanks to Mr. Peart, it is clear enough. We are told that the Hebrew word for " summer fruit" is kaitz," and the word for "end" is " ketz." "The parable is made far more impressive by the pun," says the writer, " and much more liable to stick in people's memories. Just read the verse, substi- tuting the original words, 'And he said. What seest thou? And I said a basket of kaitz. Then' said the Lord unto me, The ketz is come upon my people Israel'—and we at once see how sight and sound must have combined to arrest the attention and impress the mind of the un- thinking Israelite. The beauty of this punning parable lies in the fact that (as Pusey says) 'the symbol and the word expressing it coin- cide.' "

"How to Live Well." In a letter signed "J. Oldfield " in last week's issue it is claimed that some of the finest troops in the world to-day, hardy and untiring, are vegetarians; and the logical deduction is that our own soldiers might well be fed on rice, fur- menty, and the like. There is a good deal of misapprehension as to the habits of the present day Orientals in this respect. The suggestion is that the Eastern peoples can live, thrive, and do strenuous manual or physical labour on a ha.ndful of meal or rice,- and a little milk or water, and hence that they can live at a quar- ter the cost of Europeans similarly circum- stanced. lam authoritatively assured that that is not so that the yellow populations re- quire precisely the same amount of proteids and other strengthening elements of food that we do, and without them they whuld be quite as in- capable of work as we. But what gives colour to the false statement referred to above is the fact that they use means other and less ex- pensive than we do to attain their ends. One of the greatest living authorities on dietetics (Dr. Robert Hutchison) has publicly declared that there is no relation between national char- acter and national diet, and supports his pro- position by reference to the fact that some of the cruellest nations in the world's history have been vegetarians—the allusion presumably being to the great campaigns of the corn-fed Ciesarian hosts which the correspondent now in question commends as examples for our imita- tion. One of the favourite arguments of the vegetarian " fanatic " (I thank thee, worthy councillor, for that word) is that the flesh-eater soon betrays the characteristics of the animal upon which he feeds; and at least one eminent tragedian has acted upon the hint; but, as Dr. Hutchison observes, Charles Kean profoundly imagined ;vain things when he used to regulate his diet according to the parts he had to play. For the role of tyrant he would train on pork chops; beef was his menu when he would play the murderer, and roast mutton was his food for love. Of course that was all nonsense.

Marriage Gifts. The repeated mention of wedding presents in these columns reminds one of two or three ap- propriate tit-bits. During the last month or so war "catches" have been fairly common; but in this line "peace hath her victories no less renowned." Take one example: "We've been having a regular clearance at home," explained Mr. X. at his office, "throwing all sorts of old things away. I put one of my wedding presents on the fire this morning." "Did you really?" asked a horrified colleague; "what was it?" "A copper kettle," replied X. The other day the son of an acquaintance of mine was mar- -1 d, and I sent a pair of fish servers. Of course, my little offering was duly acknowledged; equally, of course, it had been duplicated—the young Couple lust setting up housekeeping had two sets of fish servers. But that's nothing, bless you. per instance:—Father : "You seem to look at things .. plias. very different light since your marriage. newly-married daughter: to, a "Well, I ought receiving fourteen lamps and nine candelabra for wedding pres- ents." BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Amusing Discussion on Cupid and

the Doctors.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday morning, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR RELIEF.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell asked what the Guardians were to do in the matter of relief for the wives and dependents of sailors and soldiers who were serving their country. If application were made to any member of the Board had he to refer the applicant to the re- lieving officer? He had a case on Saturday night ' of two wives whose husbands had been called to the colours. He had to do his best for them, and he would like to know, in the event of such cases occurring again, from where they would get the money. Mr. Mather: He was on a Special Committee appointed at the Rural District Council and was authorised to see that the relief was given in a proper manner. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Is it expected that I should relieve them out of my own pocket? To where do I apply? The Chairman: It has nothing to do with us. It is a matter for the committee on which you were appointed. The Clerk: The relieving officer has power to deal with cases on behalf of the Lancashire Relief Committee. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Then he is the proper person to whom people should be sent?

116. Bent: I am sure'all-eases will be met.

The Chairman: The matter is out of order now. We have not quite got into working order yet.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
PHTHISICAL PATIENTS. No Shelters to be Provided.

Recently it was reported that at a meeting of the House Visiting Committee the Medical Offi- cer gave bils opinion on the question of the pro- vision of open-air shelters for phthisical patients and that after consideration it was decided that his remarks be referred to the Building Com- mittee. The last-named committee now reported that after consideration of Dr. Hodgkinson's report, and the cost estimated by the architect, they had come to the conclusion that at the present time it was not expedient to erect shel- ters as suggested. The minutes were confirmed. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: How was it that that conclusion was arrived at? I understood that the need was pressing and that the buildings were to relieve the very undesirable pressure. The Chairman You should have said that before the minutes were approved. You had better not go any further. Mr. McKinley (to the Rev. A. M. Mitchell) : The war, you know. (Laughter.)

ATTRACTION OF THE NURSES' UNIFORM.

- Young Doctors Pierced by Cupid's Darts. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell moved: "That the Guardians advertise for a resident assistant medical officer, at a salary of .000 per annum, and that lady doctors be invited to apply." He was sure they would all agree that the question was by no means a new one. The matter was serious and they were having a difficulty in getting an officer, because of the shortage of house doctors. One of them had left because he did not get an increase in salary. Dr. Night- ingale had left for reasons that had not been stated. The temporary arrangement was satis- factory for the time being, but when. it went on for weeks and months the situation was becom- ing very serious. They had to deal not only with possibilities, but with probabilities, and the probability was that if they had a severe winter there would be an extra demand for the services of the doctor. In addition to that they would have to remember that the war would inevitably produce a great deal of poverty and sickness. That would mean a further call on the hospital, for the soldiers, who would be sent home in considerable numbers, would have to be nursed. The hospital was also a training school, and, if a resident medical officer were not provided, the institution would be struck off the lists of training schools, and that would be a serious matter. He was sure Dr. Hodgkinson would welcome an experienced resident medical officer. Then came the question of salary. He had been asked "Why .000?" and his answer was for the reason that it sounded more euphonious and nicer. (Laughter.) Three hundred pounds seemed to tickle the 'ear more than £250. Of course, .000 would be the maxi- mum, but if they could get a good man at £250 they should do so. There was also the (pitstion of the lady doctor. He did not think they would get a male doctor really worth having and likely to stay with them at a salary of less than .000. They might perhaps get a lady, but he did not see why a lady should be offered less than a man. So far as his observation went, it seemed' to him that it would be a very wise thing to try a lady doctor in that institution. The young fellows had proved easy victims to Cupid's darts. (Laughter.) The nurses' uniform had proved rather too powerful an attraction for the young men coming from the hospitals. Mr. McKinley: Is there no time limit?

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: There is not.

A Member: Oh yes, there is! Mr. Dale: Patience! (Laughter.) The Rev. A. M. Mitchell : 1 think it is desir- able that a lady should be here, and then there would be no difficulty over the action of Master Cupid. (Laughter.) Miss Broadbent seconded the resolution. Mr. Knowles: How much more is the proposed salary than what we have been paying? The Chairman: Half as much again. Mr. Knowles: Only £150? I propose an amendment. Mr. Brooks: The hospital is being well and satisfactorily served by outside men. There might be something in the question of the training school. We do not want a man to come here and settle down, but to come and go. Mr. McKinley: The question of salary has been a sore point. Through not advancing the salary we have lost some very useful men. Mr. Garnett: When this matter came up two months ago it was thought that at the end of the holiday season we should be able to get hold of a good man at a reasonable figure. To-day, however, it is a question of supply and demand. As soon as these young men have finished their locum work, if they .have any spark of human-

ity in them they will all go to the front. Hcw much are we entitled to give a doctor? Can we offer £l,OOO per year? (Laughter.) Does the Local Government Board put no restriction on the salary? The Chairman: It will be left to the common- sense of the Guardians.

_ Mr. Garnett: I support Mr. Mitchell. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: You do not suggest there is any lack of common-sense? The Chairman: The matter has been consid- ered by the House Committee. The prevailing conditions were thought to be satisfactory. We are working under abnormal conditions, and in a few weeks' time we shall get back to normal conditions. There is no necessity for this reso- lution.

.—.-... Miss Broadbent: After the explanations given I am willing to let the matter stand over for a few weeks. On the resolution being put to the meeting two voted for it and fourteen against.

WARRIOR VAGRANTS? Suggested Use for "Never Works."

The Sedgefield Union asked the Guardians to support the following resolution: "That in view of the large number of able-bodied vagrants recently admitted to the wards of Workhouses, between the ages of 18 and 30, who appear to be generally living on the community the attention of the Local Government Board and the War Office be drawn to the matter, with a view to having legislation passed for the purpose of util- ising their services in some way for the country's good at this time of national stress." A Member: Leave it over.

Mr. McKinley: I think if they won't work they ought to fight. The Master: We have none of the men men- tioned in our institution. Mr. Brooks: If they won't work they won't fight.

Mr. Mather: If you got the laziest man imag- inable he would kill five Germans befare be was killed himself.

THE EXAMINER, S ATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 12,1914.

"A NATION IN ARMS."

Present-Day Needs as Seen by

Alderman Bennett.

CURIOUS ASSERTIONS.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—l have long been a believer in the neces- sity of National Service, and my opinions have been abundantly confirmed and justified by the events of the last few weeks. To-day, the land which closed its eyes so stupidly to obvious dangers, and its ears to the warnings of some of its best and greatest men, would probably accept the principle without demur. But it seems to be considered inadvisable to raise a controversial issue at the present time, and the Government are apparently satisfied that the aim in view may be attained without resorting to this drastic measure. Whether they are right or not, the end alone will show. In my opinion, if we had been wise enough to take Lord Roberts's advice, we should not now be engaged in a struggle for life and death. But I quite admit that, if we can really secure "A' Nation in Arms" without compulsion, it is better so. And England seems, at last, to be awakening to the greatness of the need, and everywhere her sons are rallying to the cause. 11 The few who are still disposed to loaf and shirk 1 and idle are beginning to feel very much ashamed of themselves, and everyone is eagerly asking, "What can I do?"

===== 0002601_19140912_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MATERIAL AVAILABLE.

It seems to me that some elastic and compre- hensive scheme should be at once devised to make the utmost use of all the fine material at our disposal. And I would begin at the very bottom with the Boy Scouts. They are proving their utility on every hand to-day. Apart from their immediate usefulness, the training and discipline they undergo is quite invaluable. In every town or village where a troop or a patrol does not exist, it should at once be organised, and every healthy lad of proper age should join. I read the other day that in Germany; in face of the.grave national emergency, every youth between the age of 16 and 21 is being trained in the use of the rifle. Why not apply the same principle here to all who are too old to join the Boy Scouts and too young to enter Kitchener's Army?

For those over 19 and under 35, who are phy- sically fit, and who are not prevented by any other overmastering necessity, the path is clear. Recruits are flocking to the Flag in steadily in- creasing numbers, and the inspiring process can be greatly stimulated by such happy schemes as that Lord Derby launched last week in Liver- pool.

If there are some who, whilst not prepared to enter Kitchener's Army, are still anxious to join the colours, there remain the Territorials. Some seventy regiments have volunteered, I understand for foreign service. The places they have left vacant should be filled at once, for it must not be forgotten that Lord Haldane estimated that we require a Territorial Army of at least three hundred thousand men for home defence.

Correctly interpreting the popular feeling, the Mayor has announced that he intends to call a public meeting with the object of forming a Civilian Army. Many of us have been anxiously awaiting some such step, and have only been deterred from making a move in the matter because the formation of these organisations was not encouraged by the responsible authori- ties. Moreover, we have seen how the Kaiser appears to consider that it is a crime for a man to defend his home and all he holds most dear and that, on the approach of the German Army, the civic guards of Belgium have been disarmed and disbanded. In order to avoid any risk of such a contretemps in England, we might, I think, adopt another method. I have recently had exceptional opportunities of study- ing the Ulster and the Irish Volunteers. In less than twelve months, the Irish people have raised a quarter of a million men. A propor- tionate response from the rest of the United Kingdom would mean an army of some two and a half millions. During my last visit to Ire- land I saw the Volunteers actually equipped and armed as soldiers, and it seems to me that if we could raise a number of similar regiments here, the Government, by a stroke of the pen, could convert them into an army and so relieve them from all the disabilities which appear to attach to Civic Guards as usually understood. The men who were eligible for a Civic Guard would be equally eligible for the purpose indi- cated. I have seen lads of fifteen or so and old men well on for seventy marching side by side in Ireland, all animated with the same high spirit. The Preparatory Training Corps in- augurated at the Conservative Club with so much enthusiasm on Monday last, and kindred organisations would no doubt gladly merge themselves into the wider scheme, and affiliated companies might well be formed in all the adja- cent villages. The Special Constables in every place would form an admirable nucleus for such a force.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ESSENTIAL POINT.

But we should need to differentiate. It might, perhaps, be wise at the outset, not to enrol any body under 35, as it is of paramount importance not to interfere in any way with the recruiting for the Regular Army. Of those above that age who are physically fit for service there might be two divisions, all between 35 and 45, say, being eligible for service in any part of the United Kingdom. At the meeting held on Monday, an age limit of 50 was suggested. Thel men who were over 45 could be reserved for the defence of their Own immediate neighbourhood. And any who were over the suggested age, or! not physically strong enough to stand the strain of all the usual drill, might, at any rate, have I an opportunity of learning how to shoot, whilst there are numerous other ways in which they might render useful service. The essential point is to make everybody feel that he owes a duty to the nation, and to provide all necessary' channels for the practical expression of the spirit of patriotism which is burning in all Eng- lish breasts to-day. These are, of course, but crude suggestions, but the principle that underlies them is that every man who is willing to serve his country in any capacity whatever should find the neces- sary means to hand; that no service should be refused which is likely to be of any real value; and that, when this horrible war is over, every one of us, from the youngest to the oldest, shall be able to say that in the time of England's peril we each, according to our several oppor- tunities, were proud and glad to play our part. —Faithfully yours,

ARTHUR BENNETT. Warrington, 4th September, 1914. [lt will be seen that Alderman Bennett's letter was written last week. For want of space we had, reluctantly, to hold it over till to-day.— The Emma.]

===== 0002601_19140912_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====

An Enthusiastic Meeting Inaugurates a

Training Corps.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
DEMOCRACY AGAINST. •A TYRANT.

"This meeting approves of the proposal to form a preparatory training corps for instruc- tion in drill and musketry, and pledges itself to give the scheme its enthusiastic support." Such was a resolution passed unanimously by a large attendance of men in the Church Room, Stockton Heath, on Monday evening, when the Chairman of the Parish Council (Councillor W. Mclntyre) presided. On the platform were Aldermen Bennett, Councillor H. Roberts, the Revs. J. Collins and G. W. Clarke, Messrs. E. J. Hall, J. Whitley, J. Hallows, H. J. Westbrook, J. H. Clarke, F. Pearson, F. Warburton, Thos. Westbrook, F. Rose, and E. Bradshaw.

mained not merely unbroken, but as full of determination, with God's mercy, to bring about a successful issue as they were when they first entered French soil three or four weeks ago. We were not a decadent race. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140912_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
No Politics Now.

Another element our enemies depended on to a great extent was the fact that there were times when those in opposite political camps said and thought things about each other that were not as friendly and pleasant as theykmight be. But now all our ranks were closed up. (Applause.) They were not Liberals, Conser- vatives, Unionists, Radicals, Tariff Reformers, or Free Traders nowadays. No, thank God, they were Britishers every one of them! (Ap- plause.) He was grateful to the present leader of the Conservative party for the fact that on Friday he voiced the feelings of all English people, and lie (the speaker) hoped the Govern- ment would give adequate expression to what he said. That was that the wife, child or , dependents of any man who felt the call of duty and was ready to lay down his life should not suffer one jot or one tittle so far as money could make good their loss. They had, how- ever, wives and children to fight for, and those things stood in their hearts in a manner in which families and homes were not allowed to stand in the hearts of the men of other nations. Then came the question as to what was their duty. All of them should do something, even if they had reached his age. There were men in that room who, besides being physically eligible for the Army, were not responsible in other directions, and it was their duty to offer themselves at once. It was the desire of every-- one to take his part in one of the most fateful and one of the most magnificent defences of the common spirit of humanity; of that religion in which they all believed; of that recognition of life and all it meant throughout the world, and of determination to put down that which claimed to have domination over the bodies of men, and had brought about the most cursed and fiendish things ever heard of in history. The call came to the young men of the nation in such a way that it could not be evaded. They must lose no time in fighting under the banner of old England. (Hear, hear.) There was not one in that room who was not prepared to make great sacrifices; all of them, from the oldest to the youngest, to do that duty which lay nearest at hand, no matter what it cost them in time, money, or material. They had no need to fear, if they did their duty, but what they would live to see the dawning of that mag- nificent day when the Continent and the world would be freed for ever from a tyranny that was fiendish and disgraceful in the extreme. Councillor Roberts then proposed the resolu- tion quoted above.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
DUTY!

In seconding, Alderman BENNETT said there was only one thing to do now, and that was " duty." He was never prouder of his country- men than he was that day. He had thought we had become a little bit flabby and decadent and that we were getting too fond of pleasure and sport, and were neglecting the qualities that made England great. He now admitted that lie bad misjudged his countrymen. As soon as there were any signs of danger the old spirit came back like a flood, and everyone seemed anxious to do something to promote the cause of England and the freedom which was linked up with England, and the policy of England. (Hear, hear.) Never in the whole history of the country had they fought in a more just cause than the one for which they were now fighting. (Hear, hear.) He had seen Germany and the Germans, and was more than ever con- vinced that underneath the very thin veneer of civilisation affected by that race they found the barbarian naked and unabashed. If they had only listened to the warnings of men like Lord Roberts or Blatchford they would not be in the position they were that day. The hor- rible thing was near their doors and now that there were such great odds against them they must spend every shilling they had and send , every man they could until the victory was theirs. It was a vital matter, not only to the liberty and well-being of Europe, but to them- selves. Behind all the barbarism in Belgium and France there was a similar danger awaiting themselves. If they had allowed Germany to trample on the rights of Belgium and take France, she would have tried to crush us. (Hear, hear.) They had to stop the Germans, and, he thought, they would do it on the Continent of Europe. They had to beat the tyrant on his own ground; drive him back. through the beau- tiful places he had destroyed, so that the Rus- sian "steam rollers" could do their share and finish him. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) They must send recruits out at the earliest possible moment, and at home establish as many volun- teer forces as they could. He had seen the Volunteer Corps in Ireland marching past, and they were a fine body of men, indistinguishable from the "regulars." He was confident that the people of Warrington and district would ,respond nobly to the call and do their utmost for the good of the country. THE ARMY "ALL,RIGHT." Supporting the resolution, Mr. E. T. HALL said it was patriotic and practical. He had been spending the week-end in military sur- roundings, and had received a telegram from one of Councillor Roberts's sons, Major Richard Roberts, in which he said "The Army is all right." (Applause.) He (the speaker) had had with him a young man who was simply eating his soul out waiting for his commission and who considered the past three weeks were the longest and most miserable he had ever lived. The news which was to make things right for him finally came. His father was the last vicar of that .parish. However, the plea- santest news he had received was that a young nephew in Toronto University had received his commission in the Canadian contingent. (Ap- plause.) Everyone felt that the price would be paid, because the victory must be won. (Applause.) Everyone must do what he could and do it quickly. (Applause.) The resolution was carried with unanimity.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OPPONENT'S TRIBUTE TO THE

CHANCELLOR.

In proposing a vote of thank; to the speakers and to the organisers of the meeting Councillor H. J. WESTBROOK said he had made a solemn resolution that the first duty he performed at the first public meeting he addressed would be to say that he, who had so often criticised the Government, was prepared to recognise the most admirable manner in which they had carried out the government of the country in tonnec- tion with this war. (Loud applause.) Most particularly he singled out Sir Edward Grey, Mr. Asquith, Mr. Churchill, and Mr. Lloyd George. Mr. Lloyd George had had a very severe debit account in his (Mr. Westbrook's) books, hut a large amount had now been placed to his credit. (Applause.) He was not prepared to tell them yet on which side the balance would close. Speaking on the causes of the war, he said Germany had regarded her solemnplighted troth as a mere scrap of paper which she de- cided to throw to the winds, and to go through neutral territory. Britain did not interpret her plighted troth in that way. The honour of England was now at stake, the freedom of the whole Continent of Europe was at stake, and the freedom of the democracy of that Continent was also at stake. This was a war against demo- cracy, and one in which democracy was taking its proper part. (Applause.) Councillor J. H. CLARKE seconded, and said he entirely endorsed Councillor Roberts's words in which he supported the suggestion of the leader of the Conservative party. He would give his last halfpenny in the world to sup- port that scheme. (Cheers.) There were suffi- cient riches in the country to provide the money without the pinch being felt, but if the people as a whole had to provide it, let them do so. Perhaps it would be better for them to provide their share, so that they would feel what they were doing. He understood that the idea of forming a body of men had come from the

===== 0002601_19140912_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON'S NOBLE _RESPONSE.

" This meeting has been called because it was felt that in this grave crisis something should be done, and at once," was the opening sentence of the CHAIRMAN'S speech. Continuing, he said that in the present national crisis they must all take part to iome extent. The objects of that meeting were "To encourage recruiting for Lord Kitchener's forces," and "To consider the formation of a civic guard for the district; such guard to be composed of men ineligible for the military forces." He thought the first appeal came to everyone, and had been coming to everyone for the past few weeks. Lord Kitchener had been appealing for assistance, and His Majesty's Ministers were making appeals day by day for help. It was their duty to appeal to the people of that district for help. (Hear, hear.) The appeal came to every man, and also every woman. However, it came most strongly to those young people who were of the age to do active service. There had been a noble response in that locality, especially on the Warrington side, and also in that immediate neighbourhood. But still, as they were all aware, they were fighting a most formidable, unscrupulous and barbarous foe, and when there were so many men in the field there must be very great slaughter, which was most unfortunate. To make up those gaps in the ranks, recruits would be wanted, and it was for those present to supply whatever material they had. In regard to the second object of that meeting, he might say that that question had been taken , in hand very enthusiastically in Warrington, and he thought they had really made a start ' with what might be consideted a very sound and good object. Those present that night were to consider such a course. They were wishful that every man should do his duty and take his part, be it great or small, whatever his age might be, and that he would be of some use to the country in the present crisis. They wanted every man to consider that and come forward to help in every possible way, so that they miyi t be able to continue to sing "Britannia rules the waves." (Applause.) OUR SPLENDID TRAINING.

Mr. ROSE said they were fighting for a good cause. They had the weight of opinion on that from almost all the civilised nations of the earth. He was of the opinion that Austria was the only nation on the world's surface that was giving Germany any encouragement. He thought, however, Austria was in the fight be. cause Germany was helping her. It was very gratifying to find such a splendid response to Lord Kitchener's appeal, and he rejoiced in that 40 to 45 of his old scholars had volunteered for active service. It was a teacher's ideal that the ultimate goal of his teaching should be "peace." He had tried to educate his pupils in the prin- ciples of peace—that peace and right and not might should prevail. With such teachings it was no wonder that the conduct and morale of the men at the front was so good. He hoped that parents would impress on the children that it was not the weight of material, the actual number of men, nor the extent of mili- tary training, but rather the principles of righteousness which would succeed in the end. (Hear, hear.) They all admired the brave fellows at the front, but there was a limit to the qualifications of many of those at home, and those over the prescribed age wanted to know what they could do. There were two forces to which men over 35 years of age could belong a civil or a military force. If in the former, they would come under the Home Office as spacial constables. If branches of special con- stabulary were formed they would be in sec- tions of about twelve, with a leader to each, and at the head there would be a supreme leader. The whole would come under the local constabulary. If they decided upon a military force, they would have to arrange for the necessary drill and form a volunteer force. such as they had in Warrington. They would also have to get musketry practice, and then they would seek military recognition and become a military force. Many men in Stockton Heath had already joined the force in Warrington, and he had taken the trouble to inquire what locus stanch it had. He was informed that it had none, and that they were merely a set of civil volunteers training for a time of need. There, was not much doubt but that they would even- tually get military recognition. If they formed such a force in Stockton Heath they could affiliate with the force in Warrington and com- bine with them in their drills and mAnceuvres and have the advantage of belonging to a large body. He thought that was the best plan. An ambulance division had been formed, and all qualified men were welcome. However, they did not want men under 35 years of age, nor did they want to do anything which might pre- judice enlisting in the regular forces. The ambulance division would meet every Wednes- day in that room at 7.30.

The Relief Fund. , They had ,t 131 in hand for the relief fund and his idea was that it should be a recurrinf; decimal, so to speak, and be kept up to the mark as long as the funds were needed. He had given a subscription and he would give another one if the fund dropped to less than £6O, either from lack of subscripti,ris or the distribution of the money. (Applause,

===== 0002601_19140912_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR ROBERTS'S STIRRING

APPEAL. In the course of a most inspiring speech, Councillor H. ROBERTS said be was present at the meeting solely from a. sense of duty. (Hear, hear.). The war had touched him very closely indell, for be was the only man in his own immediate family at home. Both his sons and his son-in-law had gone with the colours. (Applause.) He had only one regret, and that was that age made it impossible for him to be with them. There were people in the country —he confessed he was one of them—who be- lieved that if they had taken the advice five years ago of Lord Roberts—(applause)—they would have had passing through the ranks of military education every able-bodied young man of a certain age. He had lived long enough to see that when the latent patriotism of the men of this country was appealed to it had never yet proved 'defaulting. They knew of the mag- nificent response which had already attended Lord Kitchener's appeal—a response accentu- ated since the news of the valour and heroism of the Expeditionary Force had been known— a response infinitely greater than the most optimistic ever expected, all because the men of England realised that there was never before in history a time of such imminent danger. The misfortune was that there were not men suffi- ciently educated from a military standpoint to come straight into the ranks. They had to make that good. It might be that the war would last a considerable time.

Awful Slaughter. They all hoped and prayed that God, in His infinite mercy, would put a stop to the awful slaughter of human beings. They had not lost their faith in the God of Battles. They still believed that the righteousness of a cause went a long way to its success, and never in the his- tory of the country had there been a time that they had gone into war with such clean hands and righteous intentions as those with which they had gone into the present one. That fact in itself was worth a whole army corps to them. In addition to that, our enemies had relied upon what had proved to be a false opinion that our race was decadent. They could not say that now in the face of the newspaper reports. Our Army had fought in a battle of retirement with magnificent courage and success, and re-

STOCKTON HEATH

Ambulance Corps.—There are now about 34 members of the Ambulance Corps formed in Stockton Heath, and a practice was held on Wednesday evening.

Relief Fund.—A sum of ,t 157 is in hand for relieving distress in the Stockton Heath, Latch- ford Without and Walton Inferior districts. There are only eight cases at present to be relieved.

• Bible Class Men in the Ranks.—We are in- formed by Mr. J. Hallows, the former leader of the Stockton Heath Men's Bible Class (Class 33). that over thirty of the members have enlisted in the Army.

Telegraph Lines.—At the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council meeting at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, a communication was received from the Postmaster-General asking permission to erect and maintain a line of telegraphs in Wal- ton Inferior.—lt was agreed to. On the Bench.—The following were the rnagis-

_ trates at the Stockton Heath Sessions held on Tuesday: Mr. A. H. Talbot (in the chair), Sir Gilbert Greenall, Councillor H. Roberts, Messrs. J. Hepherd, J. J. Whitley, W. Fletcher, F. A. Frost, C. Jackson Holmes, T. Baxter and L. Greening.

Young Men's Sacrifice.—At last Sunday's meeting of the Stockton Heath Men's Bible Class (Class B), a resolution was unanimously carried that the amount of .t 2. 10s. should be

forwarded to the relief fund. By this donation the members have deprived themselves of the usual yearly prizes. The leader of the class is Mr. Laws, the well-known Police Court mis- sioner in Warrington. Motorist Summoned.—At the Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Douglas Webb, 61, 'Chester-road, Hulme, Manchester, was charged with having driven a motor cycle at Preston Brook on August 2nd and having the identification plate at the rear obscured.—P.C. Collier said the plate was obscured by the coat of a man who was riding on the carrier.—Defendant: There was another'number-plate on the sidecar, which the policeman did not see.—The case was dismissed. Youthful Newsvendors.—James Jennings, 179, Orford-lane, Warrington, was summoned at the Stockton Heath Sessions on Tuesday for em- ploying a boy under the age of 14 years to sell newspapers after prohibited hours, i.e., 10.30 p.m.—P.C. Tudor said the lad was 12 years of age, and was selling papers in Victoria-square. In consequence of what the lad told him witness interviewed the defendant, who contended that he did not employ the boy, who was simply a customer and took his own profits.—Supt. Owen said the defendant admitted to a constable that he gave the lad some papers.—Defendant was given the benefit of the doubt, and the case was dismissed.

Drowned in Acker's Pits.—At the Stockton Heath Police Station on Saturday, Mr. H. C. Yates held an inquest on the body of James Armstrong Wilding (74), of Thelwall-lane, Latchford, whose body was found in Acker's Pits, Latchford Without, on Friday morning of last week.—The body was discovered, it was stated, at 6 a.m., lying face downwards in the shallow water, by a Latchford gardener named Ward.—lt was concluded that Wilding had fallen into the pit during the fog, and a verdict of "Found drowned" was returned.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
MARRIAGE OF MISS TATTERSALL.

A pretty wedding took place at St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, on Tuesday, Miss Amy Rebecca Tattersall, only daughter of Mr. and Mrs. F. E. B. Tattersall, of " Withycote," Alfred-street, Stockton Heath, and Mr. John Egbert Crane, of " Summerhill," Grange Mount, Birkenhead, eldest son of Mr. John Crane, being the contracting parties. The bride, who was given away by her uncle, Mr. Charles Shaw, wore a dress of white satin charmeuse, with a wreath of orange blossom and myrtle, and an embroidered veil, lent by the mother of the bridegroom. The string of old pearls she wore was the gift of her parents. She carried a bouquet of lilies.

The bridesmaids were: Miss Ada Crumble- hulme, of Bolton, cousin of the bride; Miss Mil- dred Crane, sister of the bridegroom; and the Misses Enid Shaw (of Manchester) and Barbara M. Garton, cousins of the bride. The two elder bridesmaids wore shadow-lace draped dresses of primrose chiffon taffeta, with waistbelts of royal blue moire. Their hats were of black velvet, with primrose feather mounts, and they carried bouquets of pale pink carnations. The small bridesmaids wore embroidered frocks of Swiss muslin, with shell-pink sashes and mob-caps to match. Their bouquets were of pale pink roses. The duties of best man were discharged by Mr. Richard Yates, of Liverpool; and the Rev. James Collins (vicar), assisted by the Rev. A. F. Corney, conducted the ceremony, Mr. F. Rose presiding at the organ. After a well-attended reception had been held at the Winmarleigh Cafe, Mr. and Mrs. Crane left for Llandudno, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling costume was of petrol-blue, with a cherry and black velvet hat. _ . _

The many useful and valuable presents which have been received include a case of cutlery, from the Eastern Telegraph Co. (Liverpoc:l) staff; a silver cake basket, from the St. Thomas' Sunday school teachers; a picture, from the Moore Council Schools' scholars; and silver vases from the bride's Sunday school class. The bridegroom presented the bride with a diamond and sapphire ring; the elder bridesmaids each with a pearl spray brooch, and the younger ones with brooches of ruby and pearl.

PATRIOTIC STOCKTON HEATH—continued.

Chief Constable of Cheshire, who had sent out a circular on the subject. The proposition was carried with acclama- tion.

A GOOD BARGAIN. In reply, Councillor ROBERTS said that there was a report abroad to the effect that the Ulster Volunteers—to whom he now took off his hat— had obtained a large consignment of Mauser Hiles from Germany at a ridiculously low price. The Nationalists followed their example, and it was said they were waiting for the invoice yet. kLoud laughter.) There was only one thing to be said about that, which was that it was rather a pity that those applications were not made a little earlier and more comprehensive. (Laughter and applause.) He proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman. In acknowledgment, the CHAIRMAN said that those who were prepared to give in their names could do so after the meeting. That was as far as arrangements had yet been made. Im- mediately they had the names of those who were prepared to enlist they would be called together and preliminaries would be arranged. He had conferred with Warrington and had endeav- oured to arrange for instruction in drill and for the provision of rifles. He had communi- cated with Colonel Afoggridge (of Orford Bar- racks), who stated that there was likely to be some difficulty in obtaining rifles. They could, however, apply for them, and the authorities might issue them. The Colonel also intimated that he would probably be able to secure the services of a late non-commissioned officer as drill instructor. Men of any age over 35 are at liberty to join the corps. Sixty-three volunteers came forward immedi- ately after the meeting and a few more have en- rolled since.

A SLICE OF LUCK FOR THE YORKISTS. Getting down to the opposite end of the field the Yorkshiremen had a slice of luck. A piece of faulty fielding gave Gleeson an oppor- tunity, which he lost no time in seizing. When so hard pressed that lie could hold the leather no longer, he passed it to Marshall, who succeeded in crossing over near the posts. Holland took the kick, but failed to convert. This set-back had an inspiriting effect on the Warringtonians, and they made a determined incursion into the Huddersfield territory. So persistent was their attack that the defenders were taken off their balance and made frequent mistakes. One of the mistakes afforded Dainteth an easy chance, Wildman dropping the ball practically into his hands. To get over the line under such fortun- ate circumstances was a comparatively easy matter, and Dainteth having notched the try near the posts, Brookes succeeded in landing the goal.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST HALF LEAD.

Nothing else of interest transpired in the first half, and when the teams crossed over War- rington led by four points—two goals and two tries to two tries.

Early on in the second half Warrington made a mistake, from which the Yorkshiremen were not slow to profit, Gleeson getting over at the corner. The goal shot, however, did not come off. A few minutes later Warrington got their own back. Brookes treated the crowd to a really exhilarating piece of work, as the result of which Tranter gained possession, and using his feet with rare precision scored. But when it came to the turn of the man—Brookes—who had inaugurated the onslaught to set the imprima- tur of conversion upon Tranter's try he failed, though only by inches. Towards the end of the game Rosenfeld managed to get over in the corner, and intense excitement centred round the issue of the resultant kick. It was a fateful kick, and, as it happened, it was also a lucky one for Warrington, the ball striking the cross- bar and rebanding into the field. In a min- ute or two all was over, and Warrington left the field with 13 points (two goals and three tries) to their credit, as compared with Huddersfield's 12 points (four tries).

===== 0002601_19140912_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
THRILLING LAST TEN MINUTES.

. _ The Warrington forwards all played well, their tackling. being noticeably good and of a

quality that should carry them far this season. The two halves also put up a spanking game, proving very " nippy " indeed. Brookes specially distinguished himself on the three- quarter line, whilst Baker proved exceptionally useful as a "winger." He had the redoubtable Todd as his vis-a-vis, but despite the great dis- parity in their sizes, generally managed to beat him. Jolley, the full-back, played a most re- liable game. Taken all-round, the match was an exceptionally interesting one, the last ten min- utes being of a particularly thrilling character. Warrington only held a lead of one point, and Huddersfield were attacking most desperately. Twice Rosenfeld nearly broke through, but luckily on each occasion Jolley just managed to upset his plans at the crucial moment. It might be added that the "gate" was considerably down on last year's total.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
A PROUD MOMENT.

It was a proud moment for Warrington when they stood victors on the Huddersfield ground. It was an achievement they have longed to accomplish and that they have done the trick at the beginning of the season is surely a good augury. It is the first defeat Huddersfield have experienced at home since Oldham beat them there two years ago. -

Warrington deserved their win, for they were the cleverer side, and but for hard lines on at least two occasions victory should have been theirs by a wider margin of points. During the interval between the two halves the crowd were reminded that in these days there are more serious considerations than foot- ball. The Union Jack was unfurled in the middle of the field by two Army pensioners, who stood proudly by whilst the old flag shook out its folds in the breeze. At the end of the match the spectators were invited to stay behind and listen to a few brief speeches, the burden of all of which was that every young man able to do so should join the colours and offer his services and, if need be, his life, in defence of the country that gave him birth, and had given him sustenance. The Chairman of the Huddersfield Club said there was a concensus of opinion that the game should continue. As soon as Parliament or the War Office suggested that football should stop they would close down, but up to now they thought the best course to pursue was to con- tinue. Every possible help to recruiting would be given by in the Union. the Northern Union and the clubs

OPENING GAME AT WILDERSPOOL. Warrington " A " Make a Draw. BY ,‘ LAKELANDER."

There was only a moderate attendance at Wilderspool on Saturday to witness the open- ing match of the season, when the Combination team were opposed to their near rivals from Leigh. The weather conditions were more con- ducive to cricket than football, as a powerful sun was sending forth his rays, and undoubtedly had its effect on the players. Warrington lost the toss and were called upon to face the sun and defend the railway goal. The game itself in the first half was splendidly contested in the circumstances, though Leigh were undoubtedly the smarter side, their three- quarters showing superior judgment in the handling operations. Early on Leigh were suc- cessful in "drawing first blood," this being the result of a neatly dropped goal by one of their half-backs. Later they were awarded a try, after a nice round of passtng. Warrington made several 'attempts to break through, A. Brown, Taberner and Nicholls being held up in turn, but their efforts were of no avail, and at the interval Leigh led by five points to nil. In the second In& Leigh were the first to cross the line, but again the goal kick failed. War- rington then showed to better advantage, and Nicholls got well away and enabled Taberner to gain a well-merited try. The latter, however, failed to pilot the ball over the bar. Harmer was badly obstructed, and Taberner once more took the shot at goal, but was wide of the mark. Brown, at half-back, hereabouts was playing a masterly game, and (luring one of his character- istic bursts through the pack succeeded in grounding the ball over the line, and Taberner this time landed a good goal, thus making the scores equal. Both sides made great efforts to obtain the lead, and some of the passing by the Leigh backs deserved to be rewarded. No fur- ther scoring, however, took place, the final re- sult reading: Warrington " A," one goal two tries (eight points); Leigh "A," one goal two tries (eight points).

FOOTBALL AND THE WAR. Northern Union Games to Proceed.

At a meeting of the Northern Rugby League Committee, held in Manchester on Tuesday evening, it was decided, after a couple of hours' discussion, and by a large majority, to carry out the League programme this season, and the fol- lowing recommendations were issued :

(1) That matches be played as usual, as it is impossible for all men to take up active war service, and it is thought unwise to have no relaxation from the more serious objects of life. (2) That all clubs be recommended to offer faci- lities for enlisting recruits for the Army on their grounds, both before and after a match. (3) That all clubs be asked to encourage their players to join the Army for active service, unless their employment is such that by not doing so they equally serve the country's welfare. (4) That clubs be asked not to pay any bonus to induce new players to join them, but to fill up any gaps from men available in their Own districts, even though they be not such talented players. (5) Let every person connected with the Nor- thern Union think first of the nation's honour, and need.

Mr. Foster was present as representing the Warrington Club, and, addressing the delegates, made a strong point of Warrington being a military depot, from which troops of all classes were being sent away daily, and that, in conse- quence, there was a probability that the Wil- derspool ground would be required for military purposes. He further urged that as the South Lancashires, whose headquarters are at War- rington, had borne the brunt of some of the heaviest of the fighting at the front, and there were several families in the town mourning the loss of relatives, it seemed incongruous to go on with the game this season. As another point, he mentioned the fact that the "gate" last Saturday was considerably below the average of previous seasons, whilst there was also a falling off in members' subscriptions. Personally, he could not help feeling that so long as games were played and places of amusement were kept open, the young men of the nation would not realise the serious danger to the country. If games were discontinued no doubt a greater number would be induced to join the colours. Later on the St. Helens representative moved a resolution in favour of the suspension of the League games, Mr. Foster seconding. The Chairman (Mr. Cook, of Wakefield), speaking in favour of going on with the season's arrangements, pointed out that certain of the Northern Union clubs had to contend with the sister code—as, for instance, in Hull, Leeds and Bradford—and if the Northern Union games were abandoned it would simply mean that Rugby supporters would go over to Association. Warrington, St. Helens, Leigh and Wigan voted in favour of suspension, but is Widnes, Runcorn and all the Yorkshire clubs were against the proposition, it was decided to carry on.

The final ties in the Warringkon Works Bowl- ing Association's annual main were played on Saturday on the green adjoining the Horse and Jockey Hotel. In the subsequent speech-mak- ing many references were made to the war and the part Warrington bowlers were playing in it. Lady Greenall was present to make the presen- tations.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON ATHLETIC CLUB.

Many Members With the Colours.

There was a fair muster of members at the Warrington A.C. headquarters on Monday evening, when the annual meeting was held. A great many members have joined the colours in various branches of the service, and several are already at the front, yet an effort is to be made to go on as usual. Mr. Bennett's reports, as secretary and treas- urer, well deserved the vote of thanks accorded to him. After making much needed improve- ments, the season opens with a balance in hand of £ll 16s. Bd. This result was chiefly due to the sterling help accorded by the special sports committee and the generosity of Mr. T. Roberts and Messrs. Eustance and Co., in connection with the Bank Holiday sports, which showed all ultimate profit of £l4 15s. sd. Hearty thanks were also tendered to the guarantors. The officers for the ensuing season were elected as follow : President, Sir Gilbert Greenell ; bon. auditor, G. H. Anderton; hon. secretary and treasurer, R. Bennett, 194, Gorsey-lane,. War- rington; assistant hon. secretary, H. James; captain, W. Bolton; vice-captains, F. Antrobus and H. Holbrook; committee, A. Bailie, R. Beaman, C. Berry, C. Dagnall, G. Evans, W. James, H. K. Jepson, F. Mumby, W. Price, T. Roberts, J. Tavlin, G. Wakefield, J. Walker and T. Whalley. The vice-president will be elected by the committee, who are also empowered to arrange for the opening date. Before the war started the club were pledged to take part in three massed runs of the St. Helens and District Association, on October 3rd, at St. Helens; November 7th, at Warrington ; December 12th, at Prescot.

Juniors between the ages of 16 and 19 years may now join for an annual subscription of one shilling.

It was agreed to render the Recretary assist- ance in connection with the Athletic Volunteer Force, which has already proved a prolific re- cruiter for Kitchener's Army.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
TO RETAIN OUR FREEDOM.

Mr. Patrick, the hon. secretary, in thanking Lady Greenall for her presence, said it was eight or nine years since she first came amongst them, and• although she had not been able to visit them on many occasions in the meantime, he could assure the gathering that her interest was as keen as ever. They regretted that their President—Captain Crosfield—was unable to be with them, but it was certain that he was with them in mind if not in body. His interest in the Association was such that he could not have been absent only for the fact that he had heard the call of King and country. (Hear, hear, and applause.) That call was theirs also. (Ap- plause.) They had always had a social function with speech-making at the conclusion of other seasons, but the committee on this occasion had, he believed, very wisely cancelled all the social gatherings they had in hand. They could not possibly expect to sit down and enjoy songs and merry speeches when their brothers were fight- ing at the front for the existence of the country. (Applause.) They sometimes heard that work- ing men had not much to gain or lose by the war. "We have everything to lose," exclaimed Mr. Patrick, "We have our freedom to retain, the freedom our forefathers fought for." (Ap- plause.). One Per Cent. Sufficient.

If they only believed one per cent. of the stories of cruelties that were narrated in the newspapers it was sufficient to create in the minds of all who were healthy and strong and, within the age limit an ambition to join Kitchener's army and fight for the country they loved. (Applause.) They could not stop the Kaiser if they stayed at home! (Hear, hear.) Already they had 500 men who had been mem- bers of their Association during the last five years in the ranks of the Territorials or Lord Kitchener's Army. This, he thought, was very good indeed, but there were others who would have to stay at home, and it was up to such members to see that the dependents who were left behind did not want; it was up to them to see that the children were well provided for. He was hoping that in the near future theirs and the Caledonian Associations would be able to make arrangements to sell mementoes so that they could raise a weekly sum to be forwarded to the Prince of Wales' Fund. (Applause.) Lady Greenall then presented the prizes, each recipient being heartily cheered.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
'MONSTROUS REBEL.

Mr. Willett, in proposing a—v—o—te— of thanks to Lady Greenall, referred to the " monstrous rebel who had dragged them into that unholy war." However, he had to be faced, but as the secre- tary said, they could not all go to the front. Still, they could do something in the way of caring for those left behind. They did not expect for one minute that working men could give their £1 or 10s., but they could all give their coppers, and it was wonderful how they mounted up! He recommended the course that had been inaugurated at some of the works in town of having systematic weekly collections on behalf of the relief funds. Mr. Burdett, who seconded, said that the money which was being saved by the abandon- ment of the social function was to be handed to the relief fund. They hoped the war would have a very quick ending, but of one thing they were certain—England would come out on top! (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140912_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT OF THE OTHERS?

The vote having been cordially carried, Lady Greenall, in reply, said it was a privi- lege to be amongst them, for she was always happy when amongst the worki•iii. had heard with pleasure of the 500 members of the Association who had gone to the front, but what of the eligible young members who were still left behind? How would they face their comrades when •they came back? While the men were away their women and children would be looked after. There were many married men and many single men at the front, but many more were needed if we and our Allies were to come out on top. (Applause.) GRANT TO THE TERRITORIALS. The Association has generously made a grant of £5 12s. 6d. to the local Territorial Equipment Fund.

OPEMMG MATCE I TIIE.. LOCAL LEAGUE.

ACTIVITY OF THE JUNIORS.

On Saturday the weather was ideal for the opening matches of the season in our local League, and a very good start was made as compared with the opening date of last season. There were four matches played, and the ver- dict rested with the home club in three in- stances, whilst the fourth game was. drawn. The game between the Central and the Mission Lads was a close and keenly-contested affair, and it was well on in the second half when the Mission equalised. Neither side could gain the lead again. Hollins Green were visited by the Y.M.C.A. lads, and the latter went off with great dash, and but for the steady defence of the home backs their citadel must have fallen. But once the homesters settled to their game the result was never in doubt.

The most interesting and striking feature of Saturday's matches was the success of the two Fairfield clubs, both of whom last season did not register a win until nearly half-way through their fixture lists. On Saturday, however, the United proved their superiority over the Whits Star team in unmistakable fashion, winning by four clear goals. Whilst the A.F.C. had to struggle hard for points on their opponents' ground, they proved themselves to be slightly the better team, and just deserved the win they scored. Although the Latchford Committee have, un- fortunately, had to withdraw their applications from the North Cheshire and the Warrington Leagues, owing to so many of their players having enlisted in His Majesty's forces during the present crisis, they managed to get a team together to oppose an eleven selected from the I Royal Welsh Fusiliers, who are encamped at the Locks. Neither side was fully representative, I but a good crowd assembled to see the match, with the result that the committee expect to be able to forward about £2 to the Mayor's Terri- torial Funds.

Notes by " Linesman.”

William Plaenix, Hughen, Ifazeldine, Wells and Lamb.

Fairfield: Taylor; Johnson and Roberts; Webb, Osborne and Jackson; Lawless, Chal- linor, Monks, Hayes and Bates. At the commencement of this game there was a 'fair number of spectators, including a sprinkling of ladies. In the early stages the visitors were the more aggressive, and Monks, receiving from the left, sent in a shot which gave Clayton no chance, thus registering the first point. Neither set of forwards combined at all well, owing to the good defence on both sides, and at the interval the score was in favour of the visitors by one goal to nil. The second half was very similar to the first, the backs on both sides easily defeating the forwards, and both goalkeepers having very little to do. When the final whistle sounded the result was :—Fairfield, one goal; Elmwood Cons., _nil.

A collection was made on tlle ground for tiie Territorial Fund.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

CENTRAL v. EARLESTOWN ST. JOHN'S MISSION.

The above teams opposed each other in Vic- toria Park in the opening fixtures of the season, the following players lining up Central: Roberts; Tulley and Glover; Gould- ing, W. Kilgariff and Guest; S. Hands, Yates, T. Kilgariff, White and Brown. St. John's Mission: Massey; Spurling and James; Taylor, Williams and Theobald; Wallis, Stead, Windoes, Plumpton and Almond. Referee, S. C. Essenhigh. The home captain won the toss for choice of ends, and Windoes set the ball rolling, and the opening stages were evenly contested. The visitors' gpal was the first to be seriously in danger, anti Massey saved in good style. Hands, for the homesters, opened the score with a good shot. This reverse nettled the visitors, and they quickly transferred play to the home quarter, ' and spoilt several good opportunities by getting offside. Still, they continued to have the best of the play, and the home defenders were hard pressed and conceded a corner to the visitors, who, however, failed to improve on it. Roberts had to save several good shots, but half-time arrived with the home team leading by one goal to none. On resuming, the game was keenly contested, and the ball travelled quickly from end to end. The visitors' left wing showed up most promin- ently at this stage, and one of the home backs gave away a penalty kick. Windoes was en- trusted with the kick, but failed to score. Fol- lowing this let-off, the play was in the visitors' half, but the home forwards spoilt chances by infringing the offside rule, and after a spell of mid-field play the visitors again became aggres- sive, and Theobald made the scores equal with a fast shot. From now to the finish the game was fast and keen, each team striving in vain to gain the lead, and at the sounding of the final whistle the score was one goal each. HOLLINS GREEN v. EARLESTOWN Y.M.C.A. These teams opened their season's fixtures on the ground of the former, when the following players turned out . _

Hollins Green: A. Perrin; Atherton and Kil- shaw; Buckley, Adams and Cleworth ; H. Perrin, Mellor, Martin, Taylor and Atkinson. Y.M.C.A.: Martin; T. Goulden and Lee; J. Atkinson, J. Goulden and J. Buckley; Taylor, Gilman, Fairhurst, Hughes and Riley.

Referee, J. Hadkiss. The visitors won the toss and set the home- sters to play against the wind. The President of the home club kicked off, and the visitors at once monopolised the game and pressed the home defenders very hard, but they kept their goal intact. The home team got into their stride and their forwards combined very well and made better use of their opportunities. Taylor opened the score after twenty minutes' play, and Martin soon added a second goal, the visi- tors' goalkeeper being at fault. A spell of even play followed until near the intelval, when Atkinson made the score three up with the best shot of the game. On resuming, the game was evenly contested, both sides doing some good defensive play, until Martin broke through and scored the fourth goal for the homesters. Following these reverses the visitors played more keenly and a good effort by W. Taylor reduced the visitors' arrears. Martin again scored for the home team, thus doing the "hat trick." Shortly afterwards Hughes scored again for the visitors. At the close of play the score was :—Hollins Green, five goals; Y.M.C.A., two goals. For the home team, Kilshaw, Martin and Atkinson played well, and for the visitors J. Goulden, Buckley and Fairhurst showed up best.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
FAIRFIELD UNITED v. ORFORD WHITE

STAR.

These teams met on the ground of the first- named club in their opening fixtures of this season's League campaign, the following players lining out under Referee B. Howell United: Potter; Rowlands and Woodhouse; Gleave, Bebbington and Cole; Cox, Roberts, Scott, Woodhouse and Poulter. White Star: Pickering; Harrison and Gregory; Reardon, Hatton and Ilangliton; Ret- ford, Day, Jackson, Carter and Allen.

The hOra' e team won the toss and from the kick-off Scott passed out to Cox, who beat three opponents and then centred across the goal- mouth, but the visitors' backs cleared their lines. Even play followed in mid-field, after which Roberts gained possession and sent in a shot which hit the crossbar and bounced over the goal-line, thus opening the score for the home team. At this reverse the visitors made a strong effort to capture the home citadel, llatton just failing with a good shot. Scott was successful in scoring a second goal for the United. The half-time score read :—United, two goals; White Star, nil. On resuming, the visitors gave a much- improved display, and good play by Carter and Allen placed the home goal in danger. But Rowlands and Woodhouse would not yield, and play was transferred into the visitors' quarters, when Poulter scored No. 3 for the homesters. Shortly after, Scott scored again with a splen- did shot from twenty yards' range. Thereafter, the visitors' efforts were well held in check by the home half-backs, and when time was called the score was:—Fairfield United, four goals; Orford White Star, nil. ELMWOOD-AVENUE CONS. v. FAIRFIELD. These teams opened their season's fixtures on the ground of the former, when the following players turned out, in charge of Referee W. Dennett

Elmwood Cons.: Clayton; Jos. Pltcenix and Mellor; Pritchard, J. S. Phcenix and Dutton;

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

LATCHFORD v. ROYAL WELSH FUSILIERS

Teams representing the above clubs met on Saturday on the Thelwall-lane enclosure in a friendly fixture, the proceeds being in aid of the Mayor's Territorial Fund. Neither team was fully representative, the home club only fielding three of last season's players, while the Soldiers were not able to play their best eleven owing to some of their men being called away during the week. When the teams entered the field there were about 250 spectators. The players lined out as follows : Latchford: Melton; Caldwell and Pilling; Gif- ford, Denny (captain) and Siddall ; S. Smith, Maddock, Challinor, Vernon and Owen.

Fusiliers: Jones; Pugh and Kelly (captain); Lieut. T. Jones, 131055 e and Lance-Corporal Melia; Bramwell, Lawrence, Stonehouse, Evans and Davies.

Referee, Mr. H. Smith:

The home team lost the toss and kicked off towards the lane goal. The visitors' defenders were quickly called upon. Jones, in saving 'a shot from the home right wing, failed to get the ball clear, and Owen rushed in and scored the opening goal. This served to brighten the game up, but the Soldiers could not hold the home players, and Vernon added the second point. The visitors now put more vim into the game, and, after a spell in the home territory, Brain. well secured the ball and scored a splendid goal. Vernon and Maddock added goals for the ' home team before the interval, at which the score was four goals to one in favour of the home team.

On resuming, the homesters assumed the aggressive, and further goals were quickly added, until their score was brought up to seven, after which they eased somewhat, and the Soldiers had more of the game. During their pressure on the home goal Caldwell con- ceded three penalty kicks, and ere the end was reached the visitors had so improved their posi- tion that they were only defeated by the odd goal in 13, the result being :—Latchford, seven goals; Royal Welsh Fusiliers, six.

FOOTBALL ITEMS.

Earlestown St. John's team created a good impression by their display against the C,ent-al. Windoes missed the first penalty of the season. Roberta was in fine form between the Central uprights. Theobald scored the equalising goal. The Y.M.C.A. Lads spent their force in the first quarter's play against Hollins Green. Martin (Hollins Green) is the first "bat trick" artist of the season.

Kilshaw played finely at full-back. Goulder, Buckley and Fairhurst were the pick of the visitors.

The Orford White Star showed up badly against Fairfield United. A. win by four clear goals should entitle the United players to shake hands with each other. The Elmwood—Fairfield game was a close affair.

The home team disappointed their supporters. - - - - • • •- - - - -

The Latchford Club have set the local 'clubs a fine.example by raising £2 for the Territorial Fund.

ANGLING.

Warrington 'Working Men's

Association.

N.A. CENTRE.

More than the usual number were out last week-end. Both Norton and Mickle Trafford had their fair share of members. Although there was a slight improvement in the canal at Norton, not a roach was captured to beat the one of 7oz. 10dr. already weighed in. _ _

- The Gowy also provided members with fair sport, the best of the catches coming from the bottom end. Members who visited Little Barrow length report that they did fairly well; catches of roach ranging up to 41b. and 51b. were taken. The water was very clear, and anglers could see the fish on feed, but methods had to be adopted to hook them that are known only to those who are familiar with this water.

We hear remarks weekly about shoals of gudgeon and perch being seen, especially above the mill. Well, as we remarked a few weeks ago, to account for these species being in the Gowy in quantities, we put it down to the Shropshire Union Canal being run off in the stream. As the Gowy water is so much superior to our canal waters, it is no wonder that the fish thrive in it.

RESULT OF DEPUTATION TO HARTFORD. The secretary, along with the treasurer, visited the free length above the Newbridge Salt Works, and reported on the matter to a general meeting on Monday last. It was at once seen that the length was not satisfactory to a club of the strength of the N.A. of War- rington. A club, say, thirty strong, might manage in this stretch, but, according to our secretary's sketch, it would be unfair almost to any society over that number to attempt to fish a contest, to give each individual a fair chance. THE ANNUAL MATCH. It was decided at Monday's general meeting to again fish the match in the canal at Aintree. Several places were put to the ballot, but Ain- tree was on top when the votes were counted up, and it at once met the approval of all mem- bers present. It was also decided for members to meet at the Blue Anchor by 1.15 to get their draw tickets, as the contest starts at 2 p.m prompt. Most of the members are travelling by Che- shire Lines; train starts at 9.15. The secretaries will be down at headquarters, Gladstone Arms, from 7.30 p.m. to-day, to re- ceive entries, contributions, &c. A.S.

TERRITORIAL COLLECTING BOX Charge Against a South Lancashire Private.

PRISONER REMANDED.

At the Borough Police Court on Thursday morning a charge of stealing a Territorial col- lecting box, containing 2s. 31d., was preferred against Daniel Larkin, a private in the South Lancashire Regiment. The magistrates were Mr. J. Rymer Young (in the chair), Mr. A. J. Jolley, Dr. Naden and Mr. W. Roberts.

„ Supt. Williamson said it was necessary to ask for a remand until Saturday, as it was necessary for the police to make certain inquiries. The box was taken from the shop of Mrs. Whittaker, 9, Pinners-brow, about seven o'clock the pre- vious morning. A number of similar boxes had been deposited in various buildings throughout the town, under the supervision of Mr. J. H. Smethurst, of Briery Croft, for the purpose of raising a sum of money for the local Territorials. The one placed in Mrs. Whittaker's shop was glued to the counter, and was secure when she went out at 6.30, but when she returned it had gone. The prisoner was seen with the box in his possession by a man who kept him under ob- servation and saw him go into a public-house, and who drew the attention of a soldier on police duty to the circumstances. The soldier went into the public-house, took the box from the prisoner and detained him until the arrival of P.C. Dawson. The man was under the influ- ence of drink, but when he was sober he was charged, and he stated he had nothing to say. The prisoner said he came to join the South Lancashires and did not know anything about the Offence. The remand asked for was granted.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
The Greatest Commandment.

Which is the greatest commandment? In de- livering his monthly address to the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday afternoon, the Rev. T. T. James answered the question by quoting christ's answer: "Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thine heart . . . and thy neigh- bour as thyself." To the Jews, said the speaker, religion was law; it sometimes puzzled them, since they were compelled to change as laws and customs changed. But they could not trap Jesus with their' ideas of Sabbath keeping, which were so exacting as to be contrary to all reason.

- ------- At the same time the observance of the Sab- bath in the true spirit we agreed with. One *day of rest in seven was very necessary. They were extremists who described the plucking of the ears of corn as work, and said that sandals should be without nails, lest it made labour to walk in them. Christ summed up the position: ▪ Come unto Me all ye that labour and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest." All that was needed was for us to love God and our neighbours; Christianity would sweep away the difficulties. In the present great war we had a just cause, it was true, but armament had de- stroyed much of the highest in life, when mutual love one with another should be the ruling spirit. The address was listened to throughout With great attention. Mr. B. Farrar, the secretary, presided, and Mr. C. Cotton also took part in the meeting. Two new members received a hearty welcome.

SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR " THE EXAMINER."
