===== 0002601_19140912_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
TURN OF THE TIDE.

GERMANS "IN TOUCII " VUTII PARIS, BUT LEAVE IT.

Successful Turning Move. FRANCO-BRITISII ,FORCE DRIVE BACK

THE FOE.

At the time of I;i:ilin-g it appears as though the great German assault upon France that was to capture Paris as a preliminary to the return of the Germans to face Russia has completely missed fire. The German advanced posts got in touch with the defenders of Paris, but it was only a slight touch, and since then they have been the chased and not the chasers. So far the strategy and the tactics favoured by Ger- many and France have been followed, and the battle which has raged this week over a line extending from I'aris to Verdun is the outcome, after a month of preliminaries, of the theories of the military leaders in those countries. The Germans believe profoundly in a violent and rapid offensive. Immense forces are used in the decisive direction with a reckless energy meant to force the defenders to conform to the Ger- man plan. In France also the military chiefs put their trust in attack. They, however, recog- nised years ago that Germany's necessarily more numerous armies -gave her power to seize the offensive when war began, and so establish a strategical advantage which could be destroyed only by a tactical victory. Accepting thus the consequences of a numeri- cal inferiority—the populations of the two States are 65 millions to 39 against the Republic—the French worked out their theory in time of peace. To begin with, the enemy's initiative was to be accepted of necessity, but from the beginning the defence was to be conducted so as to prepare for the moment when their advan- tage could be snatched from the Germans. That moment was the moment of the counter-attack, which, planned before the mobilisation was ordered, was, at the chosen opportunity, to be pressed with all energy. A battle then won would open the way to a final French victory. Such a theory, says the "Manchester Guardian," has been expounded by many able French writers, and the latest developments in the western theatre of war suggest that General Joffre has followed a plan dictated by such views. This is the interesting point at which we have this week arrived.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLY SUCCESSES.

On Friday last week the Military Government of Paris reported :—" The movements of the op- posing armies near Paris have been continued without contact taking place. In the Verdun region the German force sustained some checks. In Lorraine and the Vosges our troops have won fresh partial successes. The general situation has changed little."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
PARIS "NEGLECTED."

• Germans Turn Away from the Coveted City. An official communique issued by the Military Government on Saturday, referring to a mes- sage that the Germans had turned aside, said : " The German Army is still moving away from Paris and continuing the movement entered upon two days ago. It follows from the infor- mation received that the enemy's troops have evacuated the Compiegne-Senlis district."

PARIS, September O. The following official communique was issued to the Press at 3 p.m. to-day "The Allies' advanced troops defending Paris came into touch yesterday with the enemy's forces, which seemed to be covering on the Ourcq, towards the south-east, a movement of the main body of the German right wing. The small resulting engagement was to our advan- tage." An earlier communique stated that, in attempting to execute an outflanking movement, the enemy was apparently neglecting Paris. The German troops had reached La Ferte-sous- Jouarre and had passed Reims.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS BROUGHT TO A STAND.

"POWERFUL" BRITISH ASSISTANCE.

It became clear that the German right had been checked and was being driven back in a vast battle along the whole front from Paris to Verdun. The German attack had at last been held.

A communication issued at Bordeaux on Mon- day said that thanks to the vigorous action of the French troops, powerfully supported by the British Army, the German forces, which had advanced the day before yesterday as far as the Coulommiers and La Ferte-Gaucher district, were compelled to fall back in the evening.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH STATEMENT.

'On Monday night the Official Press Bureau in London issued the following : "General Joffre's plans are being steadily carried out. The Allied forces are acting on the offensive, and have been successful in checking and forcing back in a north-easterlY direction the German forces opposed to them."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS THROWN BACK TEN MILES.

From the Official Press Bureau. Tuesday, 11.30 p.m.

n. The general position continues satisfactory. The Allies are gaining ground on their left along the line of the Ourcq and the Petit Morin rivers. The British troops have here driven the enemy back ten miles. Fighting has been in progress further to the right along the line Montruirail-le- Petit-Sompuis, neither side gaining the advan- tage. Further to the right again, from near Vitry-le-Francois to Sermaise-les-Bains, the enemy has been pressed back in the direction of Reims. In the vicinity of Luneville an attempt by the Germans to advance has been repulsed.

Midnight.

The pressure against the enemy continues all along the Allies' front. The' British force has been engaged all day. The enemy opposed to it, after a stubborn resistance, retired, and is now crossing to the north of the Marne. The Fifth French Army has advanced with equal success, and reports many captures. The Sixth French Army on the Ourcq has been heavily engaged, but here also the enemy has been driven back. The German Army has suffered severely along the whole line, the advance having been every- where resolutely pushed home. The British forces have again sustained some casualties, but the number is small in relation to the nature of the fighting. The result of the two clays' operations is up to the present very satisfactory.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
MANY PRISONERS TAKEN.

From Paris we learnt that the enemy was retiring in the direction of the Marne between 3feaux and Sezanne. The Anglo-French troops had taken numbers of prisoners, including an Infantry Battalion and a quick-firer company with caissons.

THE GERMANS' MENACED WING. BORDEAUX, Wednesday. An official statement issued by the Ministry of War on Tuesday midnight stated On our left wing the Germans have crossed the Petit Morin in their retreat. In an attempt to protect their communications they have de- livered violent but fruitless attacks against those of our forces which are occupying the right bank of the Ourcq. Our British allies are pursuing their offensive in the direction of the Marne. On the plateaux to the north of Sezanne our troops are advancing, although with diffi-

===== 0002601_19140912_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESSURE SUSTAINED.

6ERAIAN REINFORCEMENTS DO NOT CIIECK OUR MEN.

Both Paris and Bordeaux sent out the most favourable reports on Wednesday. In the centre the advance was "slow but general." This news is most important, for it shows that the Germans are not only being held, hut driven back at the crucial point, where everything seems to indicate that they are mak- ing a determined effort to break through the positions of the Allies. On the left wing of the Allies there has been desperate fighting. The Germans, we are told, have reinforced their troops, but "the situation remains satisfactory. The enemy, is falling back before the British Army." "The Times" military correspondent pointed out that there were signs of Gcrman weakening on the Oise, at Beauvais, on the Somme, and even at Lille.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
VALUE OF BRITISH ARMY'S CO-OPERATION.

General Joffre, the French Commander-in- Chief, has conveyed to Lord Kitchener his high appreciation of the unfailing aid given by the British troops to the French armies. He says it is most valuable at the present moment, and is being given very vigorously in the action now in progress against the German right wing. Lord Kitchener replied : " May I ask you to accept and to forward to General Joffre my most sincere thanks for the telegram which you have been so kind as to send me? I ask you to believe and to inform General Joffre how happy the English Army is in co-operating with the French Army, and how proud we are of the worthy task of bringing them the help of which you speak in such gener- ous terms and on which you can always count with entire confidence."

===== 0002601_19140912_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS ROUTED

FLIGHT BEFORE THE BRITISH ADVANCE.

The Press Bureau yesterday afternoon issued the following : The general retreat of the enemy continued yesterday. Between prisoners and wounded the British force captured 1,400, besides several guns and Maxims, and a large quantity of transport. The enemy is retiring rapidly east of Soissons in some disorder. In a later telegram it is stated that from further information yesterday's captures were higher than was reported. Considerable bodies of Infantry are being found hiding in the woods, having been left behind owing to the rapid retirement of their forces. They surren- der at sight. This and the rifling of villages and evidences of drunkenness point to demoral- isation of the routed enemy. -

===== 0002601_19140912_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
The pursuit is being vigorously pressed. RAPID ADVANCE.

A French official report says that during the four days that the battle lasted the Allied Armies have in this part of the theatre of oper- ations gained more than 60 kilometres (374 Between Chateau Thierry and Vitry le Fran- cois the Prussian Guard has been driven back to the north of the marshes of Saint Gond. The action continues with great violence in the region between Mailly and Vitry le Francois. In the centre and on the right wing the situa- tion is stationary. On the Ornain and in Argonne, where the two adversaries maintain their positions at the side of Nancy, the enemy has advanced slightly. On the route of Chateau Satin we have as a set- off gained some ground in the Direst Champe- noux. The losses are considerable on both sides. The morale and health of our troops remain excellent.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
AFTER MONS. SIR JOIN FRENCH ON HIS GLORIOUS RETREAT.

Sir John French's first long despatch describ- ing the fighting in Southern Belgium and Nor- thern France was issued in London on Wednes- day.

lready appeared

lie tells the story, whie, .as a, _ in "The Examiner" in general outline, of the battles of Mons and Le Cateau-Cambrai. From his report it is more than ever clear how vio- lent was the attack on the British and how cer- tain their destruction would have been had the commanding officers not shown the highest skill and the troops the most desperate bravery. For one whole day four German Army Corps vio- lently assailed three British divisions, nearly 200,000 men against less than 60,000. Sir John French pays a striking tribute to the Royal Flying Corps, whose members, he says. performed the most valuable services ith the most resolute courage. By fighting in the air they destroyed five of the enemy's, aircraft.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====

VERY FEW BULLET WOUNDS. The Press Bureau has issued the following statement :

It is worthy of note that hardly any of tile wounds have been caused by rifle fire. Shrapnel bullets account for nearly all the wounds, and the great majority of those injured are not dan- gerously wounded. Loss of limbs has so far been very uncommon, if we may generalise from the conditions at Netley, and it would be true to say that the vast majority of the wounded soldiers will make good recoveries and will re- turn to their regiments. The fact that there are so very few rifle-bullet wounds seems to confirm the statement that the German Infantry aim badly.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE NORTH SEA.

H.M.S. PATHFINDER SUNK.

At half-past four last Saturday afternoon the scout cruiser Pathfinder was sunk 20 miles off the east coast of Scotland. 3t was originally reported, officially, that the cruiser was struck by a mine, but later it was unmfioiiifly reported to be the work of a submarine of the enemy. /f this is so, it is the first success of the German Navy since the war begirt. A list of casualties issued by the Admiralty shows that four seamen were killed and 13 wounded, while the " missing" numbered 242.

WILSON LINER SUNK. The Wilson liner Runo was sunk by a mine at 4.35 p.m. on Sunday, about 20 miles off the East Coast All the crew and passengers were saved except about 20 Russian emigrants. - The first-class passengers included Mr. Ray, an American Consul, on his way to take up a new post at Odessa. He was injured about ;he head, and with other first-class passengers -41. s taken to the Station Hotel. All the members of the crew were saved, and. as far as known, all the women and children were rescued. Of 238 passengers, 213 were saved.

RUSSIA CRUSHING AUSTRIA.

GREAT VICTORIES CLEAR WAY

TO BERLIN.

Last week-end a great victory was won by the Russians over the Austrian Army in Galicia. Over 200 guns, tens of thousands of prisoners, and much war material fell into the victors' hands. Russian troop,s occupied Lemberg, the third city in Austria and.an important roilway centre. . •

The Grand Duke Nicholas, Commarnier-in- Chief of the Russian Army, sent the following dispatch to the Tsar : . "With extreme joy, and thanking God. I announce to your Majesty that the victorious army under General Ruzsky captured Lemberg at 11 o'clock this morning. The army of General Brussiloff has taken Halicz. I beg your Majesty to confer on General Ruzsky, recognition of his services in tne precc-ding battles, the Fourth Class of the Order of St. George, and for the capture of Lemberg the Third Class of thessame Order, and on General Brussiloff, for his services in all the fighting. the Fourth Class of the Order of St. George." The retreat of the enemy after the defeat at Lemberg assumed the character of a disorderly flight and panic, and it was belieired that the remnant of the Second Austrian Army has now no longer any military value.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH FISHING FLEET SUNK.

The Admiralty announces that a .German squadron, consisting of two cruisers and four destroyers, has succeeded in sinking 15 British fishing boats in the North Sea. . A quantity of fish was captured and the crew of fishermen taken to Wilhelmshaven as prison- ers of war.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
A SEQUEL?

The fishing fleet was sunk on August 28th. It was on that date that the British Fleet inter- cepted and sank several German cruisers and destroyers and battered many others. On Sep- tember 4th the Press Bureau issued the follow- ing:— " According to information derived from a trustworthy source, seven German destroyers and torpedo-boats have arrived at Kiel in a damaged condition, and it is understood that others have sunk in the vicinity of the canal." As we have had no intimation of any further action at sea it is surmised that these arrivals at Kiel were the remains of the German squad- ron after the battle on the 28th.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPTURE OF GERMAN AIRMEN.

. -- A British submarine arrived at Harwich last week-end bringing a German naval iieutenant and a mechanic, who were captured in the North Sea. They were found clinging to an aeroplane which had broken down and was floating on the sea. The two men were rescued and made prisoners and after some bombs had been taken from it the aeroplane was sunk. The airmen were sent to the centre where German prisoners are de- tained. They were conducted by a military escort and walked unconcernedly to the fort, smiling and smoking cigarettes, in the presence of a large crowd. REMOVAL OF AIDS TO NAVIGATION. The Secretary to the Admiralty makes the following announcement All aids to navigation on the East Coast of England and Scotland, both by day and night, may be removed at any time and without any further warning than is contained in this an- nouncement. Hitherto all aids to navigation, lighthonse, lightship, buoy, Sze.. have been maintained all round the coast. Mariners of all nations have had this assistance.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
ATTEMPT TO BREAK THROUGH RUSSIAN

LINES.

Sanguinary fighting continued along the front from Lublin to Kholm, where the 10th Austrian Army Corps made an attempt to break through the Russian line. The Austrians were heavily repulsed, and 5,000 were made prisoners. The Russians secured various documents in which Austrian generals made urgent appeals for help from Germany. On September 4th the Russian troops contin- ued their energetic offensive along the whole Austrian line. The enemy's centre suffered most from the Russian attacks. In the district west of Krasnostawce the 45th Regiment of Infantry was surrounded and surrendered to a man with its commander, 44 officers, and 1,600 soldiers. A German division which was march- ing to the help of the Austrians was attacked on the left bank of the Vistula.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIA'S ROAD TO BERLIN.

A Memorable Date.

"Our armies on September 4th assumed the offensive along a front between the Vistula and the Bug." The date here cited from official dispatches will, says "The Times" Petrograd correspond- ent, be a memorable one in the history of the great European War. How eagerly it has been awaited, how much depends on the issue of the battle even now raging in Southern Poland, only those versed in the science of war can fully ap- preciate. For civilian comprehension it is suffi- cient to say that if the Russians overthrow the Austrian Armies here as they did at Lwow (Lemberg) and Halicz, the road to Berlin will be practically clear. The Russian reverse of August 30th and 31st near Ortelsburg, East Prussia, was exclusively of a local character, without any really impor- tant significance. The German reports concern- ing the number of prisoners and the importance of the German success have been enormously exaggerated. STILL ADVANCING.

PETROGRAD (St. Petersburg), September Bth. The following communique has been received from the Headquarters Staff : On September 6th engagements were proceed- ing along the whole Austrian front. The centre of the Austrian armies continued to retreat. Around Rawa Russka there was obstinate fight- ing with large Austrian forces. Our armies attacked strongly fortified positions at Grodek. _ _ .

Our advance- On the left 4 bank of the Vistula continues successfully.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS AGAINST RUSSIANS.

PETROGRAD, Thursday.

The following official statement is issued : German troops brought from the west have concentrated on the River Alle, whence they have begun a movement to East Murchti in great columns across the Masarian Lakes. The Russian advanced troops are falling back to the east, but are holding the enemy's ad- vance.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
IN BELGIUM.

HOME ARMY CAN STILL BITE.

We learn from Antwerp that the Germans set out in force last Friday to cut the Belgian com- munications with the coast. They were allowed to get south-west of lfalines; then the Belgians opened the dykes, flooding the country. The Germans fled, abandoning a large 'quantity of artillery, and they suffered heavy loss from the fire from the forts. The country round Ter- monde has also been inundated.

MORE VANDALISM. Dinant, the well-known Belgian holiday re- sort on the Meuse, has suffered the fate of Louvain. Because shots had, so they alleged, been fired on them from the woods behind the town, the Germans have shelled and partly burned the place. They have also put to death over 100 prominent citizens. An Ostend tele- gram asserts that because a bank manager re- fused to hand over the money he had in charge, or to open the safe, the Germans murdered him and his two sons.

===== 0002601_19140912_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ALL OR NONE PEACE. THE ALLIES KILL TIN KAISER'S MAIN HOPE.

The Press Bu'reau issued the following state- ment from the Foreign Office on Saturday after- noon

DECLARATION. The undersigned duly authorised thereto by the respective Governments hereby declare as follows The British, French, and Russian Govern- ments mutually engage not to conclude peae'a separately during the present war. The three Governments agree that when terms of peace come to be discussed- no one of the Allies will demand terms of peace without the previous agreement of each of the Aber Allies. In faith whereof the undersigned have signed this Declaration and have affixed thereto their seals. Done at London in triplicate the sth day of September, 1914. E. GREY, His Britannic Majesty's Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs. PAUL CAMBON, Ambassador Ex- traordinary and Plenipotentiary of the French Republic. BENCKENDOREF, Ambassador Ex- traordinary and Plenipotentiary of His Majesty the Emperor of Russia. It is evident that Germany was making or preparing to make overtures to France, and the announcement of the new undertaking is a counter-stroke of the very greatest importance to the Austro-German Alliance.

Special Constable's Activity. At the local Police Court on Wednesday morning, a sPecial constable—Mr. P. C. Dendy, 5 73, Knutsford-road—gave evidence of arrest- ing a woman who was creating a disturbance in Sankey-street the previous night. It was stated that the woman's conduct was very disgusting, and she was ordered to pay a fine and costs amounting to 13s. Gd., with the alternative of 14 days' imprisorunent.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON—. ELECTRICITY DEPARTMENT. The Electricity and Tramways Committee of the County Borough of Warrington invite TENDERS for CONSTRUCTION OF BRICK- WORK SETTINGS, for UNDERFEED STOKERS, at the Electricity Works, Warring- ton. Specification and Form of Tender can be obtained from the undersigned on payment of One Guinea, which will he returned on receipt of a bona-fide Tender. Five shillings each will be charged for extra copy of Specification. Tenders, addressed to the Chairman of the Electricity and Tramways Committee, Town Hall, Warrington, must be sealed with wax and endorsed "Tender for Construction of Brick- work Settings," and delivered not later than 12 o'clock noon on Tuesday, 22nd September, 1914. Tenders will only be considered from Firms who specialise in Water Tube Boiler and Stoker Settings. The lowest or any Tender will not necessarily be accepted. F. V. L. MATHIAS, M.1.E.E., Borough Electrical & Tramways Engineer. Howley, Warrington.
